\displaystyle=N^{1/3}\cdot\mathbf{E}\bigg[\bigg\|\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\in B_{N}\}}d\big(\mathbf{W}_{s}-\mathbf{X}^{N}_{s}\big)\bigg\|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=2kN^{1/3}\cdot\mathbf{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\in B_{N}\}}ds\bigg]=2kN^{1/3}\cdot\mathbf{E}[|B_{N}|].

Inserting the bound from (
3.10
) leads to (
3.11
). As
\mathbf{Y}^{N}
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion, (
3.11
) implies
\mathbf{X}^{N}
converges weakly to a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion.

Let us now settle the weak convergence of pairwise intersection times.
Observe that by Lemma
2.5
,
{4}N^{1/2}\nu([0,1])V^{ij}(\mathbf{X}^{N})
is a tight sequence in
C[0,T]
for each
i<j
.
Let us consider any limit point
(\mathbf{U},(K^{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq k})
of

(\mathbf{X}^{N}\ ,\ ({4}N^{1/2}\nu([0,1])V^{ij}(\mathbf{X}^{N}))_{1\leq i<j\leq k}).

By the property (
1.2
) of sticky Brownian motion, for each
i<j
we have that

|X^{N,i}(t)-X^{N,j}(t)|-{4}N^{1/2}\nu([0,1])V^{ij}(\mathbf{X}^{N})

is a martingale for each
N
. Thanks to the estimates in Lemma
2.4
, the above expression is uniformly integrable. Thus in the limit point, the process
|U^{i}-U^{j}|-K^{ij}
is a martingale as well. However, we have already established that
\mathbf{U}
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion. Thus, by Tanaka’s formula,
K^{ij}(\cdot)
has to be
L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}(\cdot)
.

Thus, summarizing, we have identified the limit point as
(\mathbf{U},(L_{0}^{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq k})
where
\mathbf{U}
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion and
L_{0}^{ij}(t):=L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}(t)
. This verifies part
3.2
.

Proof of
(a)
.
Let us take any
\mathcal{G}_{N}
satisfying the assumption of the theorem. We first prove that
\mathcal{G}_{N}
is tight in
C[0,T]
. Indeed, by Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, we have that

\mathbf{E}\big[|\mathcal{G}_{N}(t)-\mathcal{G}_{N}(s)|^{p}\big]\leq\mathbf{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_{s}^{t}d\langle\mathcal{G}_{N}\rangle(u)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\bigg]\leq C\cdot\mathbf{E}\bigg[\bigg(N^{1/2}\sum_{i<j}\big(V_{t}^{ij}(\mathbf{X}^{N})-V_{s}^{ij}(\mathbf{X}^{N})\big)\bigg)^{\frac{p}{2}}\bigg].

The second inequality above is due to the assumption (
3.3
). Lemma
2.5
implies that the term on the right-hand side of the above equation is bounded by
\mathrm{C}|t-s|^{p/4}
for some constant
\mathrm{C}>0
independent of
N
. This verifies the tightness of
\mathcal{G}_{N}
.

Note that (
3.5
) would be immediate from (
3.4
), because the latter would imply that any subsequence of the left side of (
3.5
) has a further subsequence which converges to the right side of (
3.5
). Let us therefore prove (
3.4
). Consider any joint limit point
(\mathcal{G},\mathbf{U},(K^{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq k})
of

(\mathcal{G}_{N}\ ,\ \mathbf{X}^{N}\ ,\ ({4}N^{1/2}\nu([0,1])V^{ij}(\mathbf{X}^{N}))_{1\leq i<j\leq k}).

By part
3.2
,
\mathbf{U}
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion and
K^{ij}=L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}
. By our moment estimates, we have uniform integrability of both prelimiting martingales
\mathcal{G}_{N}
and
\mathbf{X}^{N}
. Thus in the limit,
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathbf{U}
are martingales in their
joint
filtrations. By Proposition
2.6
and the assumption (
3.3
), we have boundedness of
p^{th}
moments (hence uniform integrability) of
\{e^{\mathcal{G}_{N}(T)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}_{N}\rangle_{T}}\}_{N\geq 1}.
Thus, in the limit,
\exp(\mathcal{G}(t)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(t))
is a martingale as well.

To complete the proof of (
3.4
), the main idea is to use (
3.3
) to prove that the martingales
\mathcal{G}_{N}
are asymptotically decoupled from the sticky Brownian motions. To be precise, we shall prove that for each
i
and for all
0\leq t\leq T

(3.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t}d|\langle X^{N,i},\mathcal{G}_{N}\rangle(s)|\bigg]=0.

Let us assume (
3.12
) for the moment and complete the proof of (
3.4
). Note that in the prelimit
X^{N,i}\cdot\mathcal{G}_{N}-\langle X^{N,i},\mathcal{G}_{N}\rangle
are martingales and (
3.12
) shows that the covariations vanish in probability. Therefore, in the limit, we can conclude that
\mathcal{G}\cdot U^{i}
is a martingale so that
\langle U^{i},\mathcal{G}\rangle=0.

Take any bounded
\mathcal{F}_{T}(\mathbf{U})
-measurable functional
H:C([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathbb{R}
. Note that
t\mapsto\mathbf{E}[H(\mathbf{U})|\mathcal{F}_{t}(\mathbf{U})]
is a martingale in the filtration of
\mathbf{U}
. As
\mathbf{U}
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion, by the martingale representation theorem we have

H(\mathbf{U})=\mathbf{E}[H(\mathbf{U})]+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{0}^{T}h_{s}^{i}dU_{s}^{i}

for some adapted
\mathbb{R}
-valued processes
h^{1},\ldots,h^{k}
. For the stochastic exponential we have

\exp\left(\mathcal{G}(T)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(T)\right)=1+\int_{0}^{T}\exp\left(\mathcal{G}(s)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(s)\right)d\mathcal{G}(s).

Using this we find that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\big(e^{\mathcal{G}(T)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(T)}-1\big)\big(H(\mathbf{U})-\mathbf{E}[H(\mathbf{U})]\big)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}e^{\mathcal{G}(s)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(s)}d\mathcal{G}(s)\bigg)\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}h_{s}^{i}dU^{i}_{s}\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}h_{s}^{i}e^{\mathcal{G}(s)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(s)}d\langle\mathcal{G},U^{i}\rangle(s)\bigg]=0,

where the last equality is due to the fact
\langle U^{i},\mathcal{G}\rangle\equiv 0
almost surely. This proves that for all bounded measurable
H
we have
\mathbf{E}[e^{\mathcal{G}(T)-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle(T)}H(\mathbf{U})]=\mathbf{E}[H(\mathbf{U})]
, establishing (
3.4
) modulo (
3.12
).

Let us now explain why (
3.12
) holds. Note that by assumption (
3.3
)

\mathcal{G}_{N}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\in B_{N}\}}d\mathcal{G}_{N}(s),

where
B_{N}
is defined in (
3.9
). By the Kunita-Watanabe inequality (see Proposition 2.14 in Chapter 3 of
[
KS14
]
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t}d|\langle X^{N,i},\mathcal{G}_{N}\rangle(s)|\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\in B_{N}\}}d|\langle X^{N,i},\mathcal{G}_{N}\rangle(s)|\bigg]

(3.13)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\in B_{N}\}}ds\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\langle\mathcal{G}_{N}\rangle(t)}\bigg].

By definition,
\int_{0}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\in B_{N}\}}ds\leq\sum_{i<j}V_{t}^{ij}
. Using assumption (
3.3
) we get that

\displaystyle\mbox{r.h.s.~of \eqref{w1}}\leq N^{1/4}\cdot\mathbf{E}\bigg[\sum_{i<j}V^{ij}_{t}\bigg]\leq N^{-1/4}\cdot\mathbf{E}\bigg[\exp\bigg(N^{1/2}\sum_{i<j}V^{ij}_{t}\bigg)\bigg]\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot N^{-1/4},

where we used
a\leq e^{a}
and then applied Lemma
2.4
in the last two bounds. This verifies (
3.12
), completing the proof of the theorem.

Proof of
(b)
.
Note that

\displaystyle\gamma_{N}(\Delta_{m}(0,T))\leq\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left[N^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{\{X^{2j-1}_{u}=X^{2j}_{u}\}}du\right].

From Lemma
2.4
we know the exponential moments for each of the terms in the product are uniformly bounded under
SB_{N^{1/2}\nu}^{(k)}
measure. Thus for all
p\geq 1
,

(3.14)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}[\gamma_{N}(\Delta_{m}(0,T))^{p}]<\infty.

Hence the laws of
\{\gamma_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
are tight, because the total mass of
\gamma_{N}
is a tight family of random variables and because
\Delta_{m}(0,T)
as defined in (
3.6
) is a compact space. Let
\left(\mathbf{U},\big(K^{ij}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\gamma\right)
be any limit point of the sequence
(\mathbf{X}^{N},{4}N^{1/2}\nu([0,1])\big(V^{ij}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\gamma_{N})
. By part
3.2
, we know
\mathbf{U}
is a standard
k
-dimensional Brownian motion and
K^{ij}=L_{0}^{ij}
. Note that the joint cumulative distribution function of the measure
\gamma
is necessarily given by
\left(\frac{\sigma}{{2}}\right)^{m}
times a product of the
K^{ij}
, where
\sigma=\frac{1}{{2}\nu([0,1])}
. Since we know that
K^{ij}=L_{0}^{ij}
, this immediately implies that
\gamma=\left(\frac{\sigma}{{2}}\right)^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{m}dL_{0}^{U^{2j-1}-U^{2j}}(u_{j})
. This establishes part
(b)
.

4.
Martingales associated to the sticky kernels

In this section, we begin our analysis of the field
\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}
by identifying some useful martingale observables which will later be used to identify the limit in Section
6
.

Definition 4.1
.

For a finite measure
\nu=\lambda+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}p_{k}\delta_{x_{k}}
on the real number line with
\lambda
atomless, we define its “square”

(4.1)

\displaystyle\nu^{Sq}:=\sum_{k}p_{k}^{2}\delta_{x_{k}}.

For a continuous function
\phi
we also denote by
(\nu,\phi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi\;d\nu
the natural (spatial) pairing. When
\phi
is smooth, we will use
\phi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime\prime}
to denote the first and second (spatial) derivatives of
\phi
respectively. We then have the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
K_{0,t}
denote the Howitt-Warren flow. Then for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, the process

S_{t}(\phi):=(K_{0,t},\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}(K_{0,s},\phi^{\prime\prime})ds

is a continuous martingale in the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\sigma(\{K_{a,b}(x,A):0\leq a<b\leq t,x\in\mathbb{R},A\mbox{ Borel}\}).
Furthermore its quadratic variation is given by

\langle S(\phi)\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}(K_{0,s}^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})ds.

From
[
SSS14
, Theorem 2.8]
, we know for each fixed
t>0
,
K_{0,t}
is atomic almost surely. Thus
K_{0,s}^{Sq}
is nonzero for almost every
s>0
.

Proof.

For the moment being, we consider a fixed realization of the kernels
\{K_{s,t}:0\leq s\leq t\}.
By
[
LJR04a
, Section 2.6]
, we can sample paths
B^{1},B^{2},B^{3},\ldots
starting from 0 in such a way that:

•

given
\{K_{s,t}:0\leq s\leq t\},
the paths
\{B^{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
are all independent.

•

given
\{K_{s,t}:0\leq s\leq t\},
the law of
B^{i}_{t}
given
(B_{u}^{i})_{0\leq u\leq s}
is
K_{s,t}(B_{s},\cdot)
for all
0\leq s\leq t.

It is clear that the joint quenched law of these paths
\{B^{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
is a deterministic function of the collection
\{K_{s,t}:0\leq s\leq t\}
, because the two bullet points automatically determine the multi-time marginal laws of any finite subcollection
\{B^{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
in terms of the kernels
K_{s,t}
. It is also clear that the
annealed
law of
(B^{1},\ldots,B^{r})
is just
r
-point sticky Brownian motion (see the discussion after Definition
1.1
). We define
\mu_{t}^{r}:=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta_{B_{t}^{i}}.
By the law of large numbers, as
r\to\infty
we have

\mu_{t}^{r}\to K_{0,t}\;\;\;\;\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\;\;\;\;\int_{0}^{t}\mu_{s}^{r}ds\to\int_{0}^{t}K_{0,s}ds

almost surely in the topology of weak convergence of Borel measures, for each
t>0
. Notice that

|(\mu^{r}_{t},\phi)-(\mu^{r}_{s},\phi)|\leq\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}|\phi(B_{t}^{i})-\phi(B^{i}_{s})|\leq\frac{\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}|B_{t}^{i}-B_{s}^{i}|

for all
s,t\geq 0
, all
r\in\mathbb{N}
, and all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. By Minkowski’s inequality and the fact that the
B^{i}
are individually Brownian motions in the annealed sense, we obtain

(4.2)

\mathbb{E}[|(\mu^{r}_{t},\phi)-(\mu^{r}_{s},\phi)|^{p}]^{1/p}\leq\frac{\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathbb{E}[|B_{t}^{i}-B_{s}^{i}|^{p}]^{1/p}=C_{p}\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}|t-s|^{1/2},

for any
p\geq 1
.
Define

(4.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{t}^{r}(\phi):=(\mu_{t}^{r},\phi)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}(\mu_{s}^{r},\phi^{\prime\prime})ds=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{0}^{t}\phi^{\prime}(B_{s}^{i})dB_{s}^{i}.

where the second equality above is due to Itô’s formula. Hence,
\widetilde{S}_{t}^{r}(\phi)
is a martingale. Clearly
|(\mu^{r}_{t},\phi)|\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}
. Combining this with (
4.2
) and the first equality in (
4.3
), one has

\mathbb{E}[|\widetilde{S}_{t}^{r}(\phi)-\widetilde{S}_{s}^{r}(\phi)|^{p}]^{1/p}\leq C_{p}\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}|t-s|^{1/2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\phi^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}|t-s|,

uniformly over
s,t\geq 0
. On any compact interval
[0,T]
the right side can be bounded above by
\mathrm{C}|t-s|^{1/2}
for some constant
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(p,T,\phi)
independent of
s,t\in[0,T]
. Since
S_{t}(\phi)=\lim_{r\to\infty}\widetilde{S}_{t}^{r}(\phi)
almost surely, this
L^{p}
bound implies that the limit
S_{t}(\phi)
is a continuous
L^{p}
-martingale for each
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.
The quadratic variations of
\widetilde{S}^{r}(\phi)
can be computed explicitly as

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{S}^{r}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\int_{0}^{t}\phi^{\prime}(B_{s}^{i})\phi^{\prime}(B_{s}^{j})d\langle B^{i},B^{j}\rangle_{s}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\int_{0}^{t}\phi^{\prime}(B_{s}^{i})^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{s}^{i}=B_{s}^{j}\}}ds=\int_{0}^{t}((\mu_{s}^{r})^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})ds.

We consider what happens to the quadratic variation as we take
r\to\infty
. By
[
SSS14
]
, we have that for each
t>0
,
K_{0,t}
is a.s. atomic.
Using this, it is easy to show that for each
s>0
, the quantity
((\mu_{s}^{r})^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})
will converge almost surely as
r\to\infty
to
(K_{0,s}^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2}).
Since
\mu^{r}_{t}
is a probability measure, we have
((\mu_{s}^{r})^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})\leq(\mu_{s}^{r},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})\leq\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}
deterministically, and likewise
(K_{0,s}^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})\leq(K_{0,s},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})\leq\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}
. Therefore the dominated convergence theorem applied on the product space
\mathbb{P}\otimes ds
gives

\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\big|((\mu_{s}^{r})^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})-(K_{0,s}^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})\big|ds\bigg]=0.

Here as always,
\mathbb{E}
is annealed expectation. In other words, the expression for
\langle\widetilde{S}^{r}(\phi)\rangle_{t}
will converge in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P})
as
r\to\infty
to the quantity

\int_{0}^{t}(K_{0,s}^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})ds.

Putting all of this together, we obtain that
S_{t}(\phi)=\lim_{r\to\infty}\widetilde{S}_{t}^{r}(\phi)
is a martingale with quadratic variation

\langle S(\phi)\rangle_{t}=\lim_{r\to\infty}\langle\widetilde{S}^{r}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}(K_{0,s}^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})ds,

where the limit is interpreted in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P}).
This completes the proof.
∎

Given the above lemma, our next goal is to derive a (local) martingale that is related to the rescaled field
\mathscr{X}^{N}
from (
1.4
) instead of
K_{0,t}
. To motivate the choice of our next martingale, we first perform some martingale calculations on a formal level.
The process
S
from Lemma
4.2
resembles an
orthomartingale
in the sense that the cross-variation vanishes whenever
\phi
and
\psi
have disjoint support, and the above lemma states that
K_{0,t}
solves the SPDE

dK=\partial_{x}^{2}Kdt+dS.

Note that if
u
solves the forced heat equation
\partial_{t}u=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}u+F
and if
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
then
e^{ax+bt}u(t,x+ct)
solves the equation
\partial_{t}v=Lv+\overline{F}
where

L=\frac{1}{2}\big(\partial_{x}-(a-c)I\big)^{2}+\big(b-\frac{1}{2}c^{2}\big)I,

\overline{F}(t,x)=e^{ax+bt}F(t,x+ct).

This makes sense even when
F
is a Schwartz distribution. Note that when
a=c
and
b=\frac{1}{2}c^{2}
then
L=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}
is unchanged.

We now formally go from
K_{0,t}
to
\mathscr{X}^{N}_{t}
defined in (
1.4
) in two steps. We first apply the above argument for
u=K
and
F=dS
, with
a=c=N^{-1/4}
and
b=\frac{1}{2}{N^{-1/2}}
. This gives us that the measure-valued process

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{N}(t,x)

\displaystyle:=e^{ax+bt}K_{0,t}(x+ct)=e^{\frac{t}{2}N^{-1/2}+xN^{-1/4}}K_{0,t}(x+N^{-1/4}t)

solves the SPDE

\partial_{t}\widetilde{K}^{N}=\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{K}^{N}dt+d{S}^{N}

where
{S}^{N}
is the orthomartingale measure defined by

d{S}^{N}(t,x)=e^{\frac{t}{2}N^{-1/2}+xN^{-1/4}}\cdot dS(t,x+N^{-1/4}t),

to be interpreted by integration against test functions. Next, we apply a diffusive scaling to
\widetilde{K}^{N}
. Let us set

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}^{N}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle:=N^{1/2}\widetilde{K}_{N}(Nt,N^{1/2}x)=N^{1/2}e^{\frac{t}{2}N^{1/2}+xN^{1/4}}K_{0,Nt}(tN^{3/4}+xN^{1/2}).

Note that
\partial_{t}\mathscr{X}^{N}_{t}=N^{3/2}\partial_{t}\widetilde{K}^{N}(Nt,N^{1/2}x)
and
\partial_{x}^{2}\mathscr{X}^{N}dt=N^{3/2}\partial_{x}^{2}\widetilde{K}^{N}(Nt,N^{1/2}x)dt
. Thus,
\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}
solves the SPDE

\partial_{t}\mathscr{X}^{N}=\partial_{x}^{2}\mathscr{X}^{N}dt+dM^{N}

where
M^{N}
is the orthomartingale measure defined by

\displaystyle dM^{N}(t,x)

\displaystyle=N^{3/2}d{S}^{N}(Nt,N^{1/2}x)=N^{3/2}e^{\frac{t}{2}N^{1/2}+xN^{1/4}}\cdot dS(Nt,tN^{3/4}+xN^{1/2}).

The above formal calculations suggest the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3
.

With
\mathscr{X}^{N}
as defined in (
1.4
), for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the processes

(4.4)

M_{t}^{N}(\phi):=\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}(\phi)-\phi(0)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\phi^{\prime\prime})ds

are continuous martingales with

\displaystyle\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\big((\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N})^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime}+N^{1/4}\phi)^{2}\big)ds

where
(\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N})^{Sq}
is defined via (
4.1
).

We remark that the
M^{N}(\phi)
are actually tight in
C[0,T]
as
N\to\infty
. This will follow from the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality as well as moment bounds on the quadratic variation that are proved in Proposition
5.6
.
Tightness will be proved in Proposition
6.13
, using moment formulas and bounds that we will derive in Section
5
. This will be crucial in solving the martingale problem for (
SHE
).

Proof.

The proof follows in a similar manner to the proof of Lemma
4.2
. Note that
\phi^{\prime}
from Lemma
4.2
has changed to
\phi^{\prime}+N^{1/4}\phi
due to the transformation of space-time
(t,x)\mapsto(Nt,N^{3/4}t+N^{1/2}x)
which is used to obtain
\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}
from
K_{0,t}
. We omit the details.
∎

Lemma
4.3
can be extended to include time-dependent test functions as well.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Consider
M_{t}^{N}(\phi)
as defined in (
4.4
). The process

(4.5)

\displaystyle R_{t}^{N}(\varphi):=M^{N}_{t}\big(\varphi(t,\cdot)\big)-\int_{0}^{t}M^{N}_{s}\big(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)ds

is a continuous martingale, and its quadratic variation up to time
t
is given by

(4.6)

\displaystyle\langle R^{N}(\varphi)\rangle_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\big((\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N})^{Sq},{N^{1/2}}(\varphi(s,\cdot)+N^{-1/4}\partial_{x}\varphi(s,\cdot))^{2}\big)ds.

Intuitively, one should think of
R_{t}^{N}(\varphi)
as being equal to
\int_{0}^{t}(\varphi(s,\cdot),dM^{N}_{s})_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},
which yields (
4.5
) by a formal integration-by-parts.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\sigma(\{K_{Na,Nb}(x,A):0\leq a<b\leq t,x\in\mathbb{R},A\mbox{ Borel}\})
. Fix
0\leq u\leq t
. By Lemma
4.3
we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[R_{t}^{N}(\varphi)\mid\mathcal{F}_{u}]=M_{u}^{N}\big(\varphi(t,\cdot)\big)-\int_{0}^{u}M^{N}_{s}\big(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)ds-\int_{u}^{t}M^{N}_{u}\big(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)ds.

Note that

\displaystyle\int_{u}^{t}M^{N}_{u}\big(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)ds=M_{u}^{N}\big(\varphi(t,\cdot)\big)-M_{u}^{N}\big(\varphi(u,\cdot)\big).

Inserting this identity back in (
4.7
), we get
\mathbb{E}[R_{t}^{N}(\varphi)\mid\mathcal{F}_{u}]=R_{u}^{N}(\varphi)
. This verifies that
R_{t}^{N}(\varphi)
is a martingale with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t}
. The claimed quadratic variation for
R^{N}_{t}(\varphi)
in (
4.6
) can be verified similarly.
∎

5.
Analysis of the quadratic martingale field

Recall the martingale
M_{t}^{N}
from (
4.4
). As explained in the introduction, in order to identify limit points of the field
M_{t}^{N}(\phi)
, one must study the quadratic variation of this martingale. In this section, we identify the leading order contribution of
\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle
as a functional which we call the
quadratic martingale field
—introduced below.

Definition 5.1
.

Recall the field
\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}
from (
1.4
), as well as the squaring operation from Definition
4.1
. For a bounded test function
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
, we define the quadratic martingale field (QMF) as

(5.1)

\displaystyle Q_{t}^{N}(\phi)

\displaystyle:=N^{1/2}\int_{0}^{t}\big((\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N})^{Sq},\phi\big)ds

\displaystyle=N^{1/2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-s\sqrt{N}+2uN^{-1/4}}\phi(N^{-1/2}(u-N^{3/4}s))K_{0,Ns}^{Sq}(du)ds,

From Lemma
4.3
, in view of the above definition, one has that

\langle M^{N}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=Q_{t}^{N}(\phi^{2})+\mathcal{E}_{t}^{N}(\phi),

where
\mathcal{E}_{t}^{N}(\phi)
is the “error term” given by

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{t}^{N}(\phi)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{t}\big((\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N})^{Sq},2N^{1/4}\phi\phi^{\prime}+(\phi^{\prime})^{2}\big)ds=Q_{t}^{N}\big(N^{-1/2}(\phi^{\prime})^{2}+2N^{-1/4}\phi\phi^{\prime}\big).

The main goal of this section is to show that only
Q_{t}^{N}
contributes to the limit (in a very specific way, see Proposition
5.3
) and that
\mathcal{E}_{t}^{N}
vanishes in the limit (Proposition
5.5
). The starting point of our analysis is a probabilistic interpretation of
Q_{t}^{N}(\phi)
. Indeed, similar to the representation (
3.2
) for
\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}(\phi)
, the field
Q_{t}^{N}(\phi)
also admits a formula in terms of a quenched expectation of
2
-point motion sampled from the environment
\omega:=\{K_{s,t}:-\infty<s<t<\infty\}
. Given a
2
-point motion sampled from the environment
\omega:=\{K_{s,t}:-\infty<s<t<\infty\}
, we may view
Q_{t}^{N}(\phi)
as

\displaystyle Q_{t}^{N}(\phi)

\displaystyle=N^{1/2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{(2)}_{\omega}\bigg[e^{-s\sqrt{N}+2N^{-1/4}X_{Ns}}\phi(N^{-1/2}(X_{Ns}-N^{3/4}s))\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{Ns}=Y_{Ns}\}}\bigg]ds

(5.3)

\displaystyle=N^{1/2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{(2)}_{\omega}\bigg[e^{-s\sqrt{N}+N^{-1/4}(X_{Ns}+Y_{Ns})}\phi(N^{-1/2}(X_{Ns}-N^{3/4}s))\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{Ns}=Y_{Ns}\}}\bigg]ds

where the second equality follows by replacing
2N^{-1/4}X_{Ns}
in the exponential with
N^{-1/4}(X_{Ns}+Y_{Ns})
due to the presence of the indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{Ns}=Y_{Ns}\}}.
The representation (
5.3
) will be used as a starting point in the next section, Section
5.1
, in extracting formulas and estimates for the QMF.

5.1.
Formulas and limits for the Quadratic Martingale Field

Recall the field
\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}
from (
1.4
). The goal of this section is to show that the limit of
Q_{t}^{N}
can be related to that of
\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}
in a precise way (see Proposition
5.3
). To do this, we first extract moment formulas of certain observables of
(\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N},Q_{t}^{N})
in terms of sticky Brownian motion in the same spirit as Lemma
3.1
.

Lemma 5.2

(Moment formulas)
.

Fix any bounded functions
\psi,\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
and
N\geq 1
. Suppose that
(X^{1},\ldots,X^{2k})
denotes a
2k
-point sticky Brownian motion with characteristic measure
N^{1/2}\nu
. Recall
\Delta_{m}(s,t)
from (
3.6
) and
\sigma=\frac{1}{{2}\nu([0,1])}
. We have the following moment formulas.

(a)

For all
t>0
, we have

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg(Q_{t}^{N}(\psi)-\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\phi)^{2}ds\bigg)^{k}\right]

\displaystyle=k!\int_{\Delta_{k}(0,t)}\mathbf{E}_{SB_{N^{1/2}\nu}^{(2k)}}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{k}e^{-s_{i}\sqrt{N}+N^{1/4}(X_{s_{i}}^{2i-1}+X_{s_{i}}^{2i})}\Upsilon_{s_{i}}^{N}(X^{2i-1},X^{2i})\bigg]\prod_{i=1}^{k}ds_{i},

where

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{u}^{N}(X,Y):=N^{1/2}\psi\big(X_{u}-N^{1/4}u\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{u}=Y_{u}\}}-\sigma\phi\big(X_{u}-N^{1/4}u\big)\phi\big(Y_{u}-N^{1/4}u\big).

(b)

For all
0\leq s<t
we have the following moment formula for the increment of the QMF

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg(Q_{t}^{N}(\psi)-Q_{s}^{N}(\psi)\bigg)^{k}\right]

\displaystyle=N^{\frac{k}{2}}k!\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{SB_{N^{1/2}\nu}^{(2k)}}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{k}e^{-s_{i}\sqrt{N}+N^{\frac{1}{4}}(X_{s_{i}}^{2i-1}+X_{s_{i}}^{2i})}\psi(X_{s}^{2i}-N^{1/4}s_{i})\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{s_{i}}^{2i-1}=X_{s_{i}}^{2i}\}}\bigg]\prod_{i=1}^{k}ds_{i}.

Proof.

Recall the probabilistic interpretation of
\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}(\phi)
and
Q_{t}^{N}(\psi)
from (
3.2
) and (
5.3
) respectively. Combining them we get that

(5.6)

\displaystyle Q_{t}^{N}(\psi)-\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\phi)^{2}ds=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}^{(2)}_{\omega}\left[e^{-s\sqrt{N}+N^{-1/4}(X_{Ns}^{1}+X_{Ns}^{2})}\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{s}^{N}(X^{1},X^{2})\right]ds

where
\mathbb{E}_{\omega}^{(2)}
denotes the quenched expectation of a
2
-point motion sampled from the environment
\omega:=\{K_{s,t}:-\infty<s<t<\infty\}
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{s}^{N}(X^{1},X^{2}):=N^{1/2}\psi\left(N^{-1/2}(X_{Ns}^{1}-N^{3/4}s)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{Ns}^{1}=X_{Ns}^{2}\}}-\sigma\prod_{i=1}^{2}\phi\left(N^{-1/2}(X_{Ns}^{i}-N^{3/4}s)\right).

Taking the
k
-th power of (
5.6
) we see that

(5.7)

\displaystyle\left(Q_{t}^{N}(\psi)-\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\phi)^{2}ds\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=k!\int_{\Delta_{k}(0,t)}\mathbb{E}^{(2k)}_{\omega}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}e^{-s_{i}\sqrt{N}+N^{-1/4}(X_{Ns_{i}}^{2i-1}+X_{Ns_{i}}^{2i})}\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{s_{i}}^{N}(X^{2i-1},X^{2i})\right]\prod_{i=1}^{k}ds_{i}.

In the above line, we have also used the fact that
\int_{[0,t]^{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}F(x_{i})dx_{i}=k!\int_{\Delta_{k}(0,t)}\prod_{i=1}^{k}F(x_{i})dx_{i}
(recall
\Delta_{k}
from (
3.6
)). Here
\mathbb{E}_{\omega}^{(2k)}
denotes the quenched expectation of a
2k
-point motion sampled from the environment
\omega:=\{K_{s,t}:-\infty<s<t<\infty\}
. Taking annealed expectation on both sides of the above equation, interchanging the order of integration and expectation, and then using the fact that the averaged law of
(X^{1},\ldots,X^{2k})
is a
2k
-point sticky Brownian motion with characteristic measure
\nu
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Q_{t}^{N}(\psi)-\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\phi)^{2}ds\right)^{k}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=k!\int_{\Delta_{k}(0,t)}\mathbf{E}_{SB_{\nu}^{(2k)}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{k}e^{-s_{i}\sqrt{N}+N^{-1/4}(X_{Ns_{i}}^{2i-1}+X_{Ns_{i}}^{2i})}\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{s_{i}}^{N}(X^{2i-1},X^{2i})\right]\prod_{i=1}^{k}ds_{i}.

The interchanging of the order of integration and expectation is permissible by Fubini’s theorem. Indeed, Fubini’s theorem is applicable as applying Lemma
2.4
, one can check that for each fixed
N
, the expectation of the absolute value of the integrand on the right-hand side of (
5.7
) is uniformly bounded as
\vec{s}
varies in
\Delta_{k}(0,t)
. Using the fact that
N^{-1/2}(X_{Ns}^{1},\ldots,X_{Ns}^{2k})
is distributed as
SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}
measure, (
5.4
) follows from the above formula. Relying on (
5.3
) alone, one can derive the formula in (
5.5
) by the exact same argument. This completes the proof.
∎

We now come to the main result of this section, which shows that the quadratic martingale field
Q_{t}^{N}
is well approximated by the integrated version of the square of
\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}
.
Loosely speaking, for each
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
we shall show as
N\to\infty

\displaystyle Q_{t}^{N}(\delta_{a})-\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\left(\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\delta_{a})\right)^{2}ds\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{2}}}{{\to}}0.

In other words, mollifying the squared field is comparable to squaring the mollified field. Since
Q_{t}^{N}
and
\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}
exist as distributions, to make sense of the above display, we work with a sequence of Gaussian test functions converging to
\delta_{a}
.

Proposition 5.3

(Limiting behavior of the QMF)
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}
and let
\xi(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x^{2}}
be the Gaussian test function and let
\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(x):=\varepsilon^{-1}\xi(\varepsilon^{-1}(x-a))
.
Then for all
t>0
and
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(Q_{t}^{N}(\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a})-\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}\big(\xi_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}^{a}\big)^{2}ds\bigg)^{2}\bigg]=0,

where as usual,
\sigma=\frac{1}{{2}\nu([0,1])}
. Furthermore, we have the bound

(5.9)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon>0\\
a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}\end{subarray}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left[1\wedge(|\log a|^{-2})\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(Q_{t}^{N}(\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a})-\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}\big(\xi_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}^{a}\big)^{2}ds\bigg)^{2}\bigg]<\infty.

We remark that the relevance of the second bound (
5.9
) is that even though we may not have convergence to 0 in (
5.8
) when
a=0
, we can still control the behavior near
a=0
by a blow-up that is polylogarithmic at worst. Note that the
|\log a|^{-2}
factor appears above only when
a\leq e
. In the proof of (
6.15
) in Theorem
6.16
below, we will see that such blow-ups are irrelevant in the limit.

The starting point of the proof of Proposition
5.3
is the moment formula in Lemma
5.2

(a)
. As noted in (
5.4
), the moments can be expressed as integrals of the expectation of certain observables under sticky Brownian motion. Recall that in Theorem
3.2
we saw that the expectation of a certain class of observables under sticky Brownian motion converges to the expectation of those observables under standard
k
dimensional Brownian motion. That theorem will be used to prove a similar convergence result for the expectations of the type of observables that appear in (
5.4
).

Proposition 5.4
.

Fix any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{k}<\infty
. Set
s_{0}=t_{0}
and
s_{2i-1}=s_{2i}=t_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
. Suppose
(X^{1},\ldots,X^{2k})
is distributed according
SB_{N^{1/2}\nu}^{(2k)}
. Let
(\phi_{i},\psi_{i})_{i=1}^{2k}
be bounded continuous functions on
\mathbb{R}
. Define

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t}):=\sum_{r=1}^{k}\sum_{2r-1\leq p<q\leq 2k}\left[L_{0}^{U^{p}-U^{q}}(t_{r})-L_{0}^{U^{p}-U^{q}}(t_{r-1})\right].

We have the following two limits:

(a)

(5.11)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{2k}e^{-\frac{1}{2}s_{i}\sqrt{N}+N^{\frac{1}{4}}X_{s_{i}}^{i}}\phi_{i}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}-N^{\frac{1}{4}}s_{i})\bigg]=\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 2k}}\bigg[e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{i=1}^{2k}\phi_{i}(U_{s_{i}}^{i})\bigg].

(b)

Let
A
be any subset of
\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. Set
B=A^{c}
and
define

(5.12)

\displaystyle E_{1}(\vec{t}):=\prod_{i\in A}e^{-\frac{1}{2}t_{i}\sqrt{N}+N^{\frac{1}{4}}X_{t_{i}}^{2i-1}}\phi_{i}(X_{t_{i}}^{2i-1}-N^{\frac{1}{4}}t_{i})\prod_{i\in A}e^{-\frac{1}{2}t_{i}\sqrt{N}+N^{\frac{1}{4}}X_{t_{i}}^{2i}}\phi_{i}(X_{t_{i}}^{2i}-N^{\frac{1}{4}}t_{i}),

(5.13)

\displaystyle E_{2}(\vec{t}):=\prod_{i\in B}e^{-t_{i}\sqrt{N}+N^{\frac{1}{4}}(X^{2i-1}_{t_{i}}+X^{2i}_{t_{i}})}\psi_{i}(X_{t_{i}}^{2i}-N^{\frac{1}{4}}t_{i})\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{i}}^{2i-1}=X_{t_{i}}^{2i}\}}.

Recall
\Delta_{k}(s,t)
from (
3.6
). For each
0\leq s<t\leq T<\infty
we have

(5.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{\frac{|B|}{2}}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}E_{1}(\vec{t})E_{2}(\vec{t})\prod_{i=1}^{k}dt_{i}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\left({\frac{\sigma}{2}}\right)^{|B|}\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 2k}}\bigg[\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dt_{i}\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dL_{0}^{U^{2i-1}-U^{2i}}(t_{i})\bigg].

Here
\int_{0}^{t}f(s)dL_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}(s)
denotes the integration of the continuous function
f:[0,t]\to\mathbb{R}
against the random Lebesgue-Stiltjes measure
dL_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}
induced from the increasing function
t\mapsto L_{0}^{U^{i}-U^{j}}(t).

The proof of Proposition
5.4
builds up on the estimates proved in Section
2
and uses Theorem
3.2
. We postpone the proof of Proposition
5.4
to Section
5.2
and complete the proof of Proposition
5.3
assuming it.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

For clarity, we split the proof into three steps. We first provide a brief outline of the steps below. Note that, given the moment formulas from Lemma
5.2

(a)
and the limit of those formulas from Proposition
5.4
, one can compute the
N\to\infty
limit of the expectation in (
5.9
) in terms of expectation of certain observables under a
4
D Brownian motion measure
(X^{1},X^{2},Y^{1},Y^{2})
. We then proceed to carefully massage this expectation formula to obtain the desired result.

In
Step 1
, we use a simple transformation to express the limit in terms of an expectation under a different
4
D Brownian motion measure:

(X^{1}-Y^{1},X^{2}-Y^{2},X^{1}+Y^{1},X^{2}+Y^{2}).

•

In
Step 2
, we show how local time heuristics (which are rigorously shown in Appendix
A
) can be used to rewrite the expectation formula in terms of a certain concatenation of Brownian bridge and Brownian motion measures.

•

In
Step 3
, we use local time estimates for concatenated processes (Lemma
A.6
) and heat kernel calculations to bound the formula obtained in the previous step. The bound obtained in this step is sharp enough to conclude the proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Step 1.
Applying Lemma
5.2

(a)
and Proposition
5.4

(b)
with
k=2
we get

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg(Q_{t}^{N}(\psi)-\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\phi)^{2}ds\bigg)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\sigma^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 4}}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\psi(X_{s_{i}}^{i}){\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(s_{i})-\phi(X^{i})\phi(Y^{i})ds_{i}\right)\right]

(5.15)

\displaystyle=2\sigma^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 4}}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\psi\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}+Y_{s_{i}}^{i})\big){\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(s_{i})-\phi(X^{i})\phi(Y^{i})ds_{i}\right)\right]

for all
\psi,\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathcal{L}_{2}
is defined in (
5.10
). The second equality in the above equation follows by observing that
X_{u}^{i}=Y_{u}^{i}
for
u
in the support of
L_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(du)
.

We shall now write
\mathbf{E}
instead of
\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 4}}
for convenience. Let us now take

\psi(x):=\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\varepsilon^{2}}}e^{-(x-a)^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}},\quad\phi(x):=\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\varepsilon^{2}}}e^{-(x-a)^{2}/2\varepsilon^{2}},

in (
5.15
). Using the identity
\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}(x)\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}(y)=\xi^{a}_{\varepsilon}((x+y)/2)\xi^{0}_{2\varepsilon}(x-y)
, we may now write (
5.15
) as

(5.16)

\displaystyle 2\sigma^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}+Y_{s_{i}}^{i})\big)\cdot\left({\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{X^{i}-Y^{i}}(s_{i})-\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}-Y_{s_{i}}^{i})ds_{i}\right)\right)\right].

Let us write
U^{i,-}:=X^{i}-Y^{i}
and
U^{i,+}:=X^{i}+Y^{i}
. Note that under
\mathbf{P}_{B^{\otimes 4}}
the four processes
U^{1,-},U^{1,+},U^{2,-},U^{2,+}
are independent Brownian motions with diffusion coefficient
2
. This enables us to view (
5.16
) as

\displaystyle 2\sigma^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left(\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{i}}^{i,+})\big)\cdot\left({\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{U^{i,-}}(s_{i})-\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{i}}^{i,-})ds_{i}\right)\right)\right]

(5.17)

\displaystyle=:2\sigma^{2}[A_{1}(\varepsilon)-A_{2}(\varepsilon)-A_{3}(\varepsilon)+A_{4}(\varepsilon)],

where

(5.18)

\displaystyle A_{1}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\,{\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{U^{1,-}}(s_{1})\,{\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{U^{2,-}}(s_{2})\right]

(5.19)

\displaystyle A_{2}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{2}}^{2,-})\,{\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{U^{1,-}}(s_{1})\,ds_{2}\right]

(5.20)

\displaystyle A_{3}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{1}}^{1,-})\,{\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{U^{2,-}}(s_{2})\,ds_{1}\right]

(5.21)

\displaystyle A_{4}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{1}}^{1,-})\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{2}}^{2,-})\,ds_{1}\,ds_{2}\right].

Step 2.
In this step we focus on each of the
A_{i}(\varepsilon)
terms separately. We drop the
\varepsilon
and write
A_{i}
for simplicity. Note that informally
{\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{U^{i,-}}(s_{i})
may be written as
\delta_{0}(U^{i,-})ds_{i}
which suggests that each of the
A_{i}
may be written in terms of Brownian bridge expectations. To this end, we introduce the function

(5.22)

\displaystyle H_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\vec{x},\vec{y}):=\mathbf{E}\left[e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\mid U_{s_{i}}^{i,-}=x_{i},U_{s_{i}}^{i,+}=y_{i},i=1,2\right]

where the above conditional expectation is interpreted as taking expectation under the measure where
U^{i,-}
(
U^{i,+}
resp.) is a concatenation of a Brownian bridge from
0
to
x_{i}
(
0
to
y_{i}
resp.) over the interval
[0,s_{i}]
and an independent Brownian motion started from
x_{i}
(
y_{i}
resp.) on
[s_{i},t]
. All Brownian objects considered are independent with diffusion coefficient
2
.

A_{1}
term:
Let us consider the
A_{1}
term from (
5.18
). Let
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{-}
be the
\sigma
-field generated by
\{U_{s}^{1,-},U_{s}^{2,-}\mid 0\leq s\leq t\}
. By the tower property of conditional expectation, we have

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\mathbf{E}\left[e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}^{-}\right]{\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{U^{1,-}}(s_{1})\,{\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{U^{2,-}}(s_{2})\right].

By Lemma
A.2
, the right-hand side of the above equation simplifies to

(5.23)

\displaystyle A_{1}=\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\mathbf{E}\left[e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\mid U_{s_{i}}^{i,-}=0,i=1,2\right]p_{2s_{1}}(0)p_{2s_{2}}(0)\,ds_{1}\,ds_{2}

where again the conditional expectation is interpreted via concatenation of Brownian bridges and Brownian motions. Note that via Brownian motion decomposition, we have

(5.24)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\cdot\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+})\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+})\mid U_{s_{i}}^{i,-}=x_{i}.i=1,2\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}H_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\prod_{i=1}^{2}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(\tfrac{1}{2}y_{i})p_{2s_{i}}(y_{i})dy_{i}

where
H
is defined in (
5.22
). Inserting the above formula back in (
5.23
) we get

(5.25)

\displaystyle A_{1}=\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}H_{s_{1},s_{2}}((0,0),(y_{1},y_{2}))\prod_{i=1}^{2}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(\tfrac{1}{2}y_{i})p_{2s_{i}}(y_{i})p_{2s_{i}}(0)\,dy_{i}\,ds_{i}.

A_{2}
and
A_{3}
terms:
Recall
A_{2}
from (
5.19
). We condition only on
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{1,-}:=\sigma\{U_{s}^{1,-}\mid 0\leq s\leq t\}
to get

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}F(U^{1,-}){\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{U^{1,-}}(s_{1})\right]

where

\displaystyle F(U^{1,-}):=\int_{0}^{s_{1}}\mathbf{E}\left[e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2})}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{1}}^{1,+}\big)\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}U_{s_{2}}^{2,+}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(U_{s_{2}}^{2,-})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t}^{1,-}\right]\,ds_{2}

Applying Lemma
A.2
again, we see that

\displaystyle A_{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[F(U^{1,-})\mid U_{s_{1}}^{1,-}=0]p_{2s_{1}}(0)ds_{2}.

where again the conditional expectation is interpreted in a similar manner. Performing a similar trick as in (
5.24
), we see that

(5.26)

\displaystyle A_{2}=\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}H_{s_{1},s_{2}}((0,x_{2});\vec{y})\left[\prod_{i=1}^{2}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}y_{i}\big)p_{2s_{i}}(y_{i})\right]\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(x_{2})p_{2s_{2}}(x_{2})p_{2s_{1}}(0)d\vec{y}\,dx_{2}\,d\vec{s}.

Similarly for
A_{3}
defined in (
5.20
) one has

(5.27)

\displaystyle A_{3}=\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}H_{s_{1},s_{2}}((x_{1},0);\vec{y})\left[\prod_{i=1}^{2}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}y_{i}\big)p_{2s_{i}}(y_{i})\right]\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(x_{1})p_{2s_{1}}(x_{1})p_{2s_{2}}(0)d\vec{y}\,dx_{1}\,d\vec{s}.

A_{4}
term:
Recall
A_{4}
from (
5.21
). Note that
A_{4}
does not involve any integration with respect to local times. Thus we may use
H
from (
5.22
) directly and tricks similar to (
5.24
) to get

(5.28)

\displaystyle A_{4}:=\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}H_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\prod_{i=1}^{2}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}y_{i}\big)\xi_{2\varepsilon}^{0}(x_{i})p_{2s_{i}}(x_{i})p_{2s_{i}}(y_{i})dx_{i}\,dy_{i}\,ds_{i}.

Step 3.
In this step, we determine convergence and provide bounds for each of the
A_{i}
terms defined at the end of
Step 1
. We claim that for each
i
,

(5.29)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}A_{i}(\varepsilon)={4}\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}H_{s_{1},s_{2}}((0,0),(2a,2a))\prod_{i=1}^{2}p_{2s_{i}}(0)p_{2s_{i}}(2a)ds_{i},

where
H
is defined in (
5.22
). Inserting this limit back in (
5.17
) verifies (
5.8
). Let us consider
A_{1}(\varepsilon)
from (
5.18
). Let us consider the integrand on the right-hand side of (
5.25
):

\displaystyle p_{2s_{i}}(0)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}H_{s_{1},s_{2}}((0,0),(y_{1},y_{2}))\prod_{i=1}^{2}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(\tfrac{1}{2}y_{i})p_{2s_{i}}(y_{i})\,dy_{i}.

Clearly as
\varepsilon\to 0
, it converges to
4p_{2s_{i}}(0)H_{s_{1},s_{2}}((0,0),(2a,2a))p_{2s_{1}}(2a)p_{2s_{2}}(2a)
.
Thus, to show (
5.29
) holds for
A_{1}(\varepsilon)
, it suffices by the dominated convergence theorem to show that for all
a\neq 0
,

(5.30)

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}p_{2s_{i}}(0)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}H_{s_{1},s_{2}}((0,0),(y_{1},y_{2}))\prod_{i=1}^{2}\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(\tfrac{1}{2}y_{i})p_{2s_{i}}(y_{i})\,dy_{i}

is dominated by some integrable function of
s_{1},s_{2}
. Towards this end, note that
H
is defined as the expectation of exponentials of local times of certain linear functions of concatenated processes introduced at the beginning of
Step 2
. In Lemma
A.6
, we study these expectations and in particular show that for all
s_{1},s_{2}\in[0,t]
and for all
\vec{x},\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
H_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\leq\mathrm{C}
for some constant
\mathrm{C}>0
that depends only on
t
. As
\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(\frac{1}{2}y)=2p_{2\varepsilon^{2}}(y-2a)
, using
H_{s_{1},s_{2}}(\vec{x},\vec{y})\leq\mathrm{C}
and then using the semigroup property of the heat kernel (i.e.,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{u}(x-b)p_{t}(x)dx=p_{u+t}(b)
) we have from (
5.25
) that

(5.31)

\displaystyle\mbox{\eqref{supbd}}\leq\mathrm{C}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\prod_{i=1}^{2}p_{2s_{i}+2\varepsilon^{2}}(2a)p_{2s_{i}}(0).

Note that the heat kernel globally satisfies the bound
p_{t}(x)\leq\mathrm{C}(t+x^{2})^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{\{t>0\}}
for some large enough constant
\mathrm{C}>0.
Consequently we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\prod_{i=1}^{2}p_{2s_{i}+2\varepsilon^{2}}(2a)p_{2s_{i}}(0)ds_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,t)}\prod_{i=1}^{2}s_{i}^{-1/2}(s_{i}+a^{2})^{-1/2}\,ds_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{C}\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,a^{-2}t)}\prod_{i=1}^{2}v_{i}^{-1/2}(v_{i}+1)^{-1/2}\,dv_{i}.

where the equality above follows by making the substitution
s_{i}=a^{2}v_{i}
. Extending the range of integration we have

(5.32)

\displaystyle\int_{\Delta_{2}(0,a^{-2}t)}\prod_{i=1}^{2}v_{i}^{-1/2}(v_{i}+1)^{-1/2}\,dv_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[0,a^{-2}t]^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}v_{i}^{-1/2}(v_{i}+1)^{-1/2}\,dv_{i}\leq\mathrm{C}\max\{1,|\log a|^{2}\}.

This verifies the integrability of the bound in (
5.31
) and consequently proves (
5.29
) for
A_{1}
. Starting from (
5.26
), (
5.27
), and (
5.28
), an analogous computation verifies (
5.29
) for
A_{2},A_{3}
and
A_{4}
respectively. This establishes (
5.8
). From the polylogarithmic bound in (
5.32
) (and its analogous counterparts for
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
,
A_{4}
) one arrives at (
5.9
). This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.5

(Error term limit)
.

For any
t\geq 0
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, we have that

(5.33)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{E}_{t}^{N}(\phi)^{2}]=0.

Proof.

Note that
\phi\phi^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2}(\phi^{2}+(\phi^{\prime})^{2})
thus

|\mathcal{E}_{t}^{N}(\phi)|\leq 2N^{1/4}\left[\int_{0}^{t}((\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N})^{Sq},\phi^{2})ds+\int_{0}^{t}((\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N})^{Sq},(\phi^{\prime})^{2})ds\right]=2N^{-1/4}\left[Q_{t}^{N}(\phi^{2})+Q_{t}^{N}(\phi^{\prime 2})\right].

Now by Lemma
5.2

(b)
and Proposition
5.4

(a)
, we see that for every
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
,
L^{2}
moments of
Q_{t}^{N}(\psi)
are uniformly bounded in
N
. Since
\phi^{2}
and
\phi^{\prime 2}
are also Schwartz functions, in view of the above inequality, we readily have (
5.33
).
∎

5.2.
Supporting estimates

In this section we prove Proposition
5.4
and establish an estimate for the moments of the increments of the quadratic martingale field which will be useful in Section
6.2
.

Proof of Proposition
5.4
.

Proof of (
5.11
).
We continue with the same notation as in the statement of the Proposition
5.4
. Let us set

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{N}^{r}(t)=\sum_{j=2r-1}^{2k}\int_{0}^{t}\bigg[1-\frac{1}{m_{j}^{r}(\mathbf{X}(u))}\bigg]dX^{j}(u)

where
m_{j}^{r}(\mathbf{x}):=\#\{i\in\{2r-1,\ldots,2k\}:x_{i}=x_{j}\}
. Note that
\mathcal{G}_{N}^{r}
are martingales of the form (
2.3
) where only the last
2k-2r
of the
2k
particles are taken into account. Define the martingale

(5.34)

\displaystyle d\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}(t):=N^{1/4}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{t\in[t_{r-1},t_{r})\}}d\mathcal{G}_{N}^{r}(t).

It turns out that the stochastic exponential of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}
is precisely the tilt that gets rid of the divergent term appearing in the expectation of the left-hand side of (
5.11
). To be precise, we have that

(5.35)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{2k}e^{-\frac{1}{2}s_{i}\sqrt{N}+N^{\frac{1}{4}}X_{s_{i}}^{i}}\phi_{i}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}-N^{\frac{1}{4}}s_{i})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[e^{\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}(T)-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}\rangle(T)}\exp\bigg(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\sum_{2r-1\leq i<j\leq 2k}\hskip-2.84544pt\int_{t_{r-1}}^{t_{r}}\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{u}^{i}=X_{u}^{j}\}}du\bigg)\prod_{r=1}^{2k}\phi_{r}(X_{s_{r}}^{r})\bigg].

Note that
N^{1/4}\mathcal{G}_{N}^{r}
satisfies the assumption (
3.3
). Consequently,
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}
satisfies the assumption (
3.3
) as well. From Theorem
3.2

(a)
, it is immediate that, as
N\to\infty
, the right-hand side of (
5.35
) converges to the right-hand side of (
5.11
). This proves (
5.11
) modulo (
5.35
).

We now turn towards the proof of (
5.35
) which is done by iteratively applying (
2.5
). We illustrate the proof for the
k=2
case only; the general case follows in the exact same manner. Let us set
k=2
and write
\mathbf{E}
for the expectation with respect to
{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}
measure. Recall that
s_{2i-1}=s_{2i}=t_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Let
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\leq T}
denote the filtration
of the
2k
-point sticky Brownian motion. Note that the expression inside the first expectation in (
5.35
) can be written as
\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{B}
where

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle:=e^{-2t_{1}\sqrt{N}+N^{1/4}\sum_{j=1}^{4}X_{t_{1}}^{j}}\prod_{i=1}^{2}\phi_{i}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}-N^{\frac{1}{4}}s_{i})

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}

\displaystyle:=e^{-(t_{2}-t_{1})\sqrt{N}+N^{1/4}(X_{t_{2}}^{3}+X_{t_{2}}^{4}-X_{t_{1}}^{3}-X_{t_{1}}^{4})}\cdot\prod_{i=3}^{4}\phi_{i}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}-N^{\frac{1}{4}}s_{i}).

\mathbf{A}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t_{1}}
. By the Markov property of sticky Brownian motion
\mathbf{E}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1}}]
is measurable with respect to
\sigma\big(X_{t_{1}}^{i})_{i=1}^{4}\big)
. Let us write

\displaystyle H\big(\mathbf{X}_{t_{1}}\big):=\mathbf{E}[\mathbf{B}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1}}].

By the tower property of conditional expectation followed by an application of (
2.5
) with
\lambda=N^{\frac{1}{4}}
,
s=0
,
t=t_{1}
,
\nu\mapsto N^{\frac{1}{2}}\nu
, and
k=4
we get

(5.36)

\displaystyle\mbox{l.h.s.~of \eqref{e.multifr}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{A}\cdot H\big(\mathbf{X}_{t_{1}}\big)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[e^{N^{\frac{1}{4}}\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}(t_{1})-N^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\rangle(t_{1})}\exp\bigg(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{u}^{i}=X_{u}^{j}\}}du\bigg)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{2}\phi_{i}(X_{s_{i}}^{i})\cdot H\big(({X}_{t_{1}}^{i}+N^{\frac{1}{4}}t_{1})_{i=1}^{k}\big)\right].

Observe that by the Markov property of the sticky Brownian motion, we have

(5.37)

\displaystyle H\hskip-1.42271pt\big(({X}_{t_{1}}^{i}+N^{\frac{1}{4}}t_{1})_{i=1}^{k}\big)\hskip-2.84544pt=\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbf{E}\hskip-2.84544pt\left[e^{-(t_{2}-t_{1})\sqrt{N}+N^{\frac{1}{4}}(X_{t_{2}}^{3}+X_{t_{2}}^{4}-X_{t_{1}}^{3}-X_{t_{1}}^{4})}\prod_{i=3}^{4}\phi_{i}(X_{s_{i}}^{i}-N^{\frac{1}{4}}(t_{2}-t_{1}))\hskip-1.42271pt\mid\hskip-1.42271pt\mathcal{F}_{t_{1}}\right]\!.\!

We use (
2.5
) with
\lambda=N^{\frac{1}{4}}
,
s=t_{1}
,
t=t_{2}
,
\nu\mapsto N^{\frac{1}{2}}\nu
, and
k=2
to get that

r.h.s. of (
5.37
)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[e^{N^{\frac{1}{4}}\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2}(t_{1},t_{2})-N^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2}\rangle(t_{1},t_{2})}\cdot\prod_{i=3}^{4}\phi_{i}(X_{s_{i}}^{i})\exp\bigg(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{u}^{3}=X_{u}^{4}\}}du\bigg)\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1}}\right],

where
g(s,t):=g(t)-g(s)
. Inserting the above expression back in (
5.36
) and again using the tower property of the conditional expectation, we derive the identity in (
5.35
).

Proof of (
5.14
).
Following the same argument as in the proof of (
5.11
), in the same spirit as (
5.35
), one has that

(5.38)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[E_{1}(\vec{t})E_{2}(\vec{t})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\Bigg[e^{\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}(T)-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}\rangle(T)}\exp\bigg(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\sum_{2r-1\leq i<j\leq 2k}\hskip-2.84544pt\int_{s_{r-1}}^{s_{r}}\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{u}^{i}=X_{u}^{j}\}}du\bigg)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{i}(X_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{i}(X_{t_{i}}^{2i})\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(X_{t_{i}}^{2i})\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{i}}^{2i-1}=X_{t_{i}}^{2i}\}}\Bigg].

Set

(5.39)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t}):=e^{\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}(T)-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}\rangle(T)},\quad\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t}):=\exp\bigg(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\sum_{2r-1\leq i<j\leq 2k}\hskip-2.84544pt\int_{t_{r-1}}^{t_{r}}\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{u}^{i}=X_{u}^{j}\}}du\bigg).

Recall
\gamma_{N}
from (
3.7
). By Theorem
3.2

(a)
and
(b)
, any limit point of the sequence

\bigg(\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N},\mathbf{X},{4}N^{1/2}\nu([0,1])\big(V^{ij}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq 2k},N^{|B|/2}\prod_{i\in B}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{i}}^{2i-1}=X_{t_{i}}^{2i}\}}dt_{i}\bigg)

is of the form

\displaystyle\left(\overline{\mathcal{G}},\mathbf{U},\big(L_{0}^{U_{i}-U_{j}}\big)_{1\leq i<j\leq k},\left({\frac{\sigma}{2}}\right)^{|B|}\prod_{i\in B}dL_{0}^{U^{2i-1}-U^{2i}}(u_{i})\right)

for some
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
with
\mathbf{E}[e^{p\overline{\mathcal{G}}(T)-\frac{p}{2}\langle\overline{\mathcal{G}}\rangle(T)}]<\infty
for all
p>0
and satisfying (
3.4
). Here
U
is standard
2k
-dimensional Brownian motion. By the continuous mapping theorem, it follows that along a subsequence we have

(5.40)

\displaystyle N^{\frac{|B|}{2}}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\hskip-5.69046ptD_{N}(\vec{t})\cdot\prod_{i\in B}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{i}}^{2i-1}=X_{t_{i}}^{2i}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}dt_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\left({\frac{\sigma}{2}}\right)^{|B|}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}e^{\overline{\mathcal{G}}(T)-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\overline{\mathcal{G}}\rangle(T)+{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dt_{i}\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dL_{0}^{U^{2i-1}-U^{2i}}(t_{i}),

where

(5.41)

\displaystyle D_{N}(\vec{t}):=\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})\cdot\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t})\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{i}(X_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{i}(X_{t_{i}}^{2i})\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(X_{t_{i}}^{2i}).

We now upgrade (
5.40
) to imply convergence of first moments. To do this, we shall show the sequence of random variables in the left-hand side of (
5.40
) is uniformly integrable. Repeatedly using Proposition
2.6
and Doob’s martingale inequality to the stochastic exponentials of each of the martingales
\mathcal{G}_{N}^{r}
appearing in the expression for
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}
we find that for all
p\geq 1
, we have

(5.42)

\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\sup_{\vec{t}\in\Delta_{k}(0,T)}\big(\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})\big)^{p}\bigg]<\infty,\quad\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\sup_{\vec{t}\in\Delta_{k}(0,T)}\big(\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t})\big)^{p}\bigg]<\infty,

and thus via the definition of
D_{N}(\vec{t})
from (
5.41
) we get

\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\sup_{\vec{t}\in\Delta_{k}(0,T)}\big(D_{N}(\vec{t})\big)^{p}\bigg]<\infty.

In view of the above bound and (
3.14
), we obtain uniform integrability for the sequence of random variables in (
5.40
). This implies that along the same subsequence,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{\frac{|B|}{2}}\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\hskip-5.69046ptD_{N}(\vec{t})\cdot\prod_{i\in B}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{i}}^{2i-1}=X_{t_{i}}^{2i}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}dt_{i}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\left({\frac{\sigma}{2}}\right)^{|B|}\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}e^{\overline{\mathcal{G}}(T)-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\overline{\mathcal{G}}\rangle(T)+{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dt_{i}\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dL_{0}^{U^{2i-1}-U^{2i}}(t_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\left({\frac{\sigma}{2}}\right)^{|B|}\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\mathbf{E}\left[e^{\overline{\mathcal{G}}(T)-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\overline{\mathcal{G}}\rangle(T)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T}(\mathbf{U})\right]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\cdot\left.\prod_{i\in A}\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i-1})\phi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dt_{i}\prod_{i\in B}\psi_{i}(U_{t_{i}}^{2i})dL_{0}^{U^{2i-1}-U^{2i}}(t_{i})\right],

where the last equality follows from the tower property of the conditional expectation. By (
3.4
), the above inner expectation is
1
. Since the limit is free of
\overline{\mathcal{G}}
, we have the same above limit along every subsequence. Thus we arrive at the (
5.14
) formula. This completes the proof.
∎

We end this section by recording a couple of useful estimates for moments of the increments of the quadratic martingale field.

Proposition 5.6

(Estimates for moments of the increments of QMF)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
T>0
. Then there exists a constant
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(k,T)>0
such that for all bounded measurable functions
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
and all
0\leq s<t\leq T
one has that

(5.43)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\big[(Q_{t}^{N}(\phi)-Q_{s}^{N}(\phi))^{k}\big]\leq\mathrm{C}\|\phi\|^{k}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}(t-s)^{k/4}.

Furthermore fix
p>1
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exists
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(p,\varepsilon,k,T)>0
such that for all functions
\phi\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
and all
\varepsilon\leq s<t\leq T
one has

(5.44)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[(Q_{t}^{N}(\phi)-Q_{s}^{N}(\phi))^{k}\big]\leq\mathrm{C}\|\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}^{k}(t-s)^{k}.

We remark that the first bound is crude and nowhere near optimality. However, the latter bound is more important, and it will be most powerful when
p
is very close to
1,
as this will allow us to obtain optimal tightness bounds for limit points in the next section.

Proof.

Proof of (
5.43
).
Using (
5.5
) together with the trivial bound
|\phi(N^{-1/2}X^{j}_{t_{j}}-N^{1/4}t_{j})|\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}},
we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\big(Q_{t}^{N}(\phi)-Q_{s}^{N}(\phi)\big)^{k}]

\displaystyle\leq N^{k/2}k!\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}e^{-t_{j}\sqrt{N}+N^{1/4}(X^{2j-1}_{t_{j}}+X^{2j}_{t_{j}})}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{j}}^{2j-1}=X_{t_{j}}^{2j}\}}\bigg]dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k}

(5.45)

\displaystyle=k!\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k}\cdot N^{k/2}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})\cdot\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t})\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{j}}^{2j-1}=X_{t_{j}}^{2j}\}}\bigg]dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k},

where in above we use the same notation from (
5.39
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t}):=e^{\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}\rangle},\quad\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t}):=\exp\bigg(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{r=1}^{k}\sum_{2r-1\leq i<j\leq 2k}\hskip-2.84544pt\int_{t_{r-1}}^{t_{r}}\hskip-2.84544pt\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{u}^{i}=X_{u}^{j}\}}du\bigg),

and where
\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}=\overline{\mathcal{G}}_{N}(t)
defined in (
5.34
). The equality in (
5.45
) is due to (
5.38
).
Note that by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and using the fact that
\mathbf{A}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{B}^{2}\leq\mathbf{A}^{4}+\mathbf{B}^{4}
, we have

(5.46)

(
5.45
)

\displaystyle\leq k!\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k}\sqrt{N^{\frac{k}{2}}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[(\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})^{4}+\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t})^{4})\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{j}}^{j}=Y_{t_{j}}^{j}\}}\bigg]dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k}}

(5.47)

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt\cdot\sqrt{N^{\frac{k}{2}}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{j}}^{j}=Y_{t_{j}}^{j}\}}\bigg]dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k}}

For the factor in the square root of (
5.47
), note that

(5.48)

\displaystyle N^{\frac{k}{2}}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{j}}^{j}=Y_{t_{j}}^{j}\}}\bigg]dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}N^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t}^{j}=Y_{t}^{j}\}}dt\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\bigg(N^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t}^{j}=Y_{t}^{j}\}}dt\bigg)^{k}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}|t-s|^{k/2}.

where the last inequality follows by applying Lemma
2.5
on each of the expectations. Here the constant
\mathrm{C}>0
depends only on
k
.
We now focus on the factor in the square root of (
5.46
). We shall show that this factor can be bounded uniformly in
N\geq 1
and
s,t\in[0,T]
. We split it into two parts: one containing
\mathbf{A}(\vec{t})
and
\mathbf{B}(\vec{t})
. Recall the
\gamma_{N}
measure from (
3.7
). Note that

\displaystyle\sup_{N\geq 1}N^{k/2}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})^{4}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{j}}^{j}=Y_{t_{j}}^{j}\}}\bigg]dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\sup_{\vec{t}\in\Delta_{k}(0,T)}\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})^{4}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}d\gamma_{N}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\sup_{N\geq 1}\sqrt{\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\sup_{\vec{t}\in\Delta_{k}(0,T)}\mathbf{A}_{N}(\vec{t})^{8}\bigg]\cdot\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\big[\gamma_{N}(\Delta_{k}(0,T))^{2}\big]}.

where the last inequality is due to Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. By (
3.14
) and by (
5.42
), we find that the above expression is finite. On the other hand, for the
\mathbf{B}(\vec{t})
term it is clear from Theorem
3.2
3.2
and
(b)
that

N^{k/2}\int_{\Delta_{k}(0,T)}\mathbf{E}_{SB^{(2k)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}\bigg[\mathbf{B}_{N}(\vec{t})^{4}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{X_{t_{j}}^{j}=Y_{t_{j}}^{j}\}}\bigg]dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k}

converges to some finite quantity as
N\to\infty
, which implies that it is bounded independently of
N
. Thus the last two math displays imply that the term in (
5.46
) is bounded uniformly in
N\geq 1
and
s,t\in[0,T]
. From (
5.48
), we see that (
5.47
)
\leq\mathrm{C}|t-s|^{k/4}
. Inserting these two bounds back in (
5.46
) and (
5.47
), we arrive at (
5.43
).

Proof of (
5.44
).
Appealing to the moment formula for the increment of QMF from (
5.5
) and the convergence from (
5.14
) we have

(5.49)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big(Q_{t}^{N}(\phi)-Q_{s}^{N}(\phi)\big)^{k}\bigg]=k!\left({\frac{\sigma}{2}}\right)^{k}\mathbf{E}_{B^{\otimes 2k}}\bigg[\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(U_{t_{j}}^{2j})dL_{0}^{U^{2j-1}-U^{2j}}(t_{j})\bigg],

where
\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})
is defined in (
5.10
).
Lemma
A.2
formalizes the intuition that
{\frac{1}{2}}dL_{0}^{U^{2j-1}-U^{2j}}(t_{j})=\delta_{0}(U_{t_{j}}^{2j-1}-U^{2j}_{t_{j}})
. Thus by appealing to that lemma, we can write the above expectation in terms of a certain family of concatenated bridge processes whose law we will write as
\mathbf{P}_{c}
. Specifically, we have that

(5.50)

\displaystyle\mbox{r.h.s.~of \eqref{e.idef}}=k!\sigma^{k}\int_{\Delta_{k}(s,t)}\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})\bigg]\prod_{j=1}^{k}p_{2t_{j}}(0)dt_{1}\cdots dt_{k},

where the expectation is taken over a collection of paths
U^{j}
such that

•

(U^{2i-1}-U^{2i},U^{2i-1}+U^{2i})_{i=1}^{k}
are
2k
many independent processes.

•

U^{2i-1}+U^{2i}
is a Brownian motion of diffusion rate
2
for
i=1,2,\ldots,k
.

•

U^{2i-1}-U^{2i}
is a Brownian bridge (from
0
to
0
) of diffusion rate
2
from
[0,t_{i}]
and an independent Brownian motion of diffusion rate
2
from
[t_{i},\infty)
for
i=1,2,\ldots,k
.

We have used a different notation for the expectation operator
\mathbf{E}_{c}
in (
5.50
) (
c
for concatenation) just to stress that the law is different from the standard Brownian motion. We claim that for all
p>1
we have

(5.51)

\displaystyle\sup_{\vec{t}\in[0,T]}\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}|\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})|\bigg]\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\|\phi\|_{L^{p}}^{k}.

where the
\mathrm{C}>0
depends on
p,k,\varepsilon,T
. Let us assume (
5.51
) for the moment. By hypothesis,
t_{j}\geq\varepsilon>0
. Thus,
p_{2t_{j}}(0)\leq\mathrm{C}
for some constant
\mathrm{C}>0
depending on
\varepsilon
. Thus, in view of (
5.51
), to get an upper bound for the right-hand side of (
5.50
), we may take the supremum of the integrand in the right-hand side of (
5.50
) and pull it outside of the integration. As the Lebesgue measure of
\Delta_{k}(s,t)
is
\frac{(t-s)^{k}}{k!}
, we thus have the desired estimate in (
5.44
).

Let us now establish (
5.51
). Fix
p>1
and take
q>1
so that
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
. Use Hölder’s inequality to write

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[e^{{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}|\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})|\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[e^{q{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\bigg]^{1/q}\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}|\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})|^{p}\bigg]^{1/p}.

For the first expectation above, observe that by Lemma
A.6
,
\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[e^{q{\frac{\sigma}{2}}\mathcal{L}_{k}(\vec{t})}\bigg]
is uniformly bounded over
\vec{t}\in\Delta_{k}(0,T)
. For the second expectation above, note that under
\mathbf{E}_{c}
, we have

U_{t_{j}}^{2j}=\tfrac{1}{2}(U_{t_{j}}^{2j-1}+U_{t_{j}}^{2j})-\tfrac{1}{2}(U_{t_{j}}^{2j-1}-U_{t_{j}}^{2j})=\tfrac{1}{2}(U_{t_{j}}^{2j-1}+U_{t_{j}}^{2j})-0.

Thus under
\mathbf{E}_{c}
,
U_{t_{j}}^{2j}
are independent Gaussian random variables with variance
t_{j}/2
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{k}|\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})|^{p}\bigg]^{1/p}=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{E}_{c}\bigg[|\phi(U^{2j}_{t_{j}})|^{p}\bigg]^{1/p}=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t_{j}/2}(y)|\phi(y)|^{p}\,dy\bigg)^{1/p}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\|\phi\|_{L^{p}}^{k}.

where the last inequality follows by again using the fact that
t_{j}\geq\varepsilon>0
, together with the uniform bound
\sup_{y}p_{t}(y)\leq(2\pi t)^{-1/2}
, and noting that the constant
\mathrm{C}
is allowed to depend on
\varepsilon
. This establishes (
5.51
) completing the proof of (
5.44
).
∎

6.
Solving the martingale problem for the SHE

The goal of this section is to establish the tightness of the field
\mathscr{X}^{N}
defined in (
1.4
) and identify its limit points as the solution of the stochastic heat equation (
SHE
). As the field
\mathscr{X}^{N}
does not exist in a functional sense, we first introduce in Section
6.1
appropriate spaces and topologies that work well with the bounds derived in the previous section. In Section
6.2
and Section
6.3
we deal with the tightness and identification of the limit points of
\mathscr{X}^{N}
respectively.

6.1.
Weighted Hölder spaces and Schauder estimates

In this subsection, we introduce various natural topologies for our field
\mathscr{X}^{N}
and its limit points. We then discuss how the heat flow affects these topologies and record Kolmogorov-type lemmas that will be key in showing tightness under these topologies. We begin by recalling many familiar and useful spaces of continuous and differentiable functions that have natural metric structures.
For
d\geq 1
, we denote by
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of all compactly supported smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define its
C^{r}
norm as

(6.1)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{r}}:=\sum_{\vec{\alpha}}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|D^{\vec{\alpha}}f(\mathbf{x})|

where the sum is over all
\vec{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{d}
with
\sum\alpha_{i}\leq r
and
D^{\vec{\alpha}}:=\partial_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{d}}^{\alpha_{d}}
denotes the mixed partial derivative.

We now recall the definition of weighted Hölder spaces from
[
HL15
, Definitions 2.2 and 2.3]
. For the remainder of this paper, we shall work with
elliptic and parabolic
weighted Hölder spaces with polynomial weight function

(6.2)

\displaystyle w(x):=(1+x^{2})^{\tau}

for some fixed
\tau>1
. We introduce these weights because weighted spaces will be more convenient to obtain tightness estimates. Since the solution of (
SHE
) started from Dirac initial condition is known to be globally bounded away from
(0,0)
, we expect that it is possible to remove the weights throughout this section, but this would require more precise moment estimates than the ones we derived in previous sections, which take into account spatial decay of the fields.

Definition 6.1

(Elliptic Hölder spaces)
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
we define the space
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the norm given by

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{|f(x)|}{w(x)}+\sup_{|x-y|\leq 1}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{w(x)|x-y|^{\alpha}}.

For
\alpha<0
we let
r=-\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
and we define
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
to be the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with respect to the norm given by

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\phi\in B_{r}}\frac{(f,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}{w(x)\lambda^{\alpha}}

where the scaling operators
S^{\lambda}_{x}
are defined by

(6.3)

\displaystyle S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi(y)=\lambda^{-1}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 6.2

(Function spaces)
.

Let
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
be as in Definition
6.1
. We define
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
to be the space of continuous maps
g:[0,T]\to C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}),
equipped with the norm

\|g\|_{C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|g(t)\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}.

Here and henceforth we will define
\Lambda_{[a,b]}:=[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}
and we will define
\Lambda_{T}:=\Lambda_{[0,T]}.

So far we have used
\phi,\psi
for test functions on
\mathbb{R}
. To make the distinction clear between test functions on
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we shall use variant Greek letters such as
\varphi,\vartheta,\varrho
for test functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In some instances, we will explicitly write
(f,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
or
(g,\phi)_{\mathbb{R}}
when we want to be clear about the space in which we are pairing.

Definition 6.3

(Parabolic Hölder spaces)
.

We define
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
to be the set of functions on
\Lambda_{T}
that are restrictions to
\Lambda_{T}
of some function in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(in particular we do not impose that elements of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
vanish at the boundaries of
\Lambda_{T}).

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
we define the space
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})
to be the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
with respect to the norm given by

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in\Lambda_{T}}\frac{|f(t,x)|}{w(x)}+\sup_{|s-t|^{1/2}+|x-y|\leq 1}\frac{|f(t,x)-f(s,y)|}{w(x)(|t-s|^{1/2}+|x-y|)^{\alpha}}.

For
\alpha<0
we let
r=-\lfloor\alpha\rfloor
and we define
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})
to be the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
with respect to the norm given by

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in\Lambda_{T}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{r}}\frac{(f,S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)_{L^{2}(\Lambda_{T})}}{w(x)\lambda^{\alpha}}

where the scaling operators are defined by

(6.4)

\displaystyle S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi(s,y)=\lambda^{-3}\varphi(\lambda^{-2}(t-s),\lambda^{-1}(x-y)),

and where
B_{r}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{r}
norm less than 1 with support contained in the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Remark 6.4

(Derivatives of distributions)
.

By definition, any element
f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
admits an
L^{2}
-pairing with any smooth function
\varphi:\Lambda_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
of rapid decay, which we can write as
(f,\varphi)_{\Lambda_{T}}.
Consequently there is a natural embedding
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
which is defined by formally setting
f
to be zero outside of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
. More rigorously, this means that the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-pairing of
f
with any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is defined to be equal to
(f,\varphi|_{\Lambda_{T}})_{\Lambda_{T}}
.

The image of this embedding consists of some specific collection of tempered distributions that are necessarily supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}.
Consequently we can sensibly define
\partial_{t}f
and
\partial_{x}f
as elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
whenever
f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
. Explicitly these derivatives are defined by the formulas

(\partial_{t}f,\varphi)_{\Lambda_{T}}:=-(f,\partial_{t}\varphi)_{\Lambda_{T}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(\partial_{x}f,\varphi)_{\Lambda_{T}}:=-(f,\partial_{x}\varphi)_{\Lambda_{T}}

for all smooth
\varphi:\Lambda_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
of rapid decay. This convention on derivatives will be useful for certain computations later. From the definitions, when
\alpha<0
one can check that for such
f
one necessarily has
\partial_{t}f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
and
\partial_{x}f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-1,\tau}(\Lambda_{T}).

We remark that this fails for
\alpha>0
. Indeed, by our convention of derivatives,
\partial_{t}f
may no longer be a smooth function (or even a function) even if
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
. This is because such an
f
gets extended to all of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by setting it to be zero outside
\Lambda_{T}
. In particular, if
f
does not vanish on the boundary of
\Lambda_{T}
, then it may become a discontinuous function under our convention of extension to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Due to these discontinuities, the distributional derivative
\partial_{t}f
can be a tempered distribution with singular parts along the boundaries (one may verify that
\partial_{t}f
can be at best an element of
C^{-2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})
for generic
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
). In our later computations, we never take derivatives of functions in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
with
\alpha>0
.

We now discuss the smoothing effect of heat flow on these weighted Hölder spaces.

Proposition 6.5

(Smoothing effect on elliptic spaces)
.

For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
t>0
define

P_{t}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}(x-y)f(y)dy.

Then for all
\alpha\leq\beta<1
, there exists
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\alpha,\beta,T)>0
such that

\|P_{t}f\|_{C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\mathrm{C}t^{-(\beta-\alpha)/2}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}

uniformly over
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}
and
t\in[0,T]
. In particular,
P_{t}
extends to a globally defined linear operator on
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
which maps boundedly into
C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}).

A proof may be found in
[
HL15
, Lemma 2.8]
in the case of an exponential weight. The proof for polynomial weights is identical.

For the parabolic Hölder spaces, the following lemma states that the heat flow improves the regularity by a factor of
2
and provides a Schauder-type estimate.

Proposition 6.6

(Schauder estimate)
.

For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
let us define

(6.5)

\displaystyle Kf(t,x):=\int_{\Lambda_{T}}p_{t-s}(x-y)f(s,y)dsdy,

where
p_{t}
is the standard heat kernel for
t>0,x\in\mathbb{R}
and
p_{t}(x):=0
for
t<0.
Then for all
\alpha<-1
with
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}
there exists
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\alpha)>0
independent of
f
such that

\|Kf\|_{C^{\alpha+2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})}.

In particular
K
extends to a globally defined linear operator on
C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})
that maps boundedly into
C^{\alpha+2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T}).
Furthermore, if
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})
, then
K(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f=f
.

We remark that the last statement is only true because of our convention on distributional derivatives that we have explained in Remark
6.4
. Without that convention, that statement would be false even for a smooth function
f
that does not vanish on the line
\{t=0\}
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi
be any smooth function that equals
1
in a ball of radius
1
about the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and is supported on a ball of radius
2
about the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
For
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
let us define
P^{+}(t,x):=p_{t}(x)\varphi(t,x)
and
P^{-}(t,x):=p_{t}(x)-P^{+}(t,x)
so that

p_{t}(x)=P^{+}(t,x)+P^{-}(t,x).

We have that
P^{-}
is a globally smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and without any loss of generality, we may assume that it is supported on
\Lambda_{T+1}
since values of the heat kernel outside
\Lambda_{T}
do not matter when convolving with distributions supported on
\Lambda_{T}
. Then we get a corresponding decomposition
K=K^{+}+K^{-}
of the operator defined in the proposition statement.

Since
P^{+}
is supported on a ball of radius 2, the proof of the weighted Schauder estimate for
K^{+}
is sketched in
[
HL15
, Page 8, proof of Corollary 1.2]
, and we do not repeat it here. That proof is given in an elliptic setting, but the proof of the parabolic version is no different because the singularity of the 1+1 dimensional heat satisfies a bound of the form

|\partial_{t}^{k_{1}}\partial_{x}^{k_{2}}p_{t}(x)|\leq\mathrm{C}(|t|^{1/2}+|x|)^{-1-2k_{1}-k_{2}},

where
\mathrm{C}
may depend on
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0
but not on
t,x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Thus it only remains to prove the weighted Schauder estimate for
K^{-}
. As
P^{-}
is globally smooth on
\Lambda_{T}
and has a Gaussian decay at infinity, we may write

P^{-}(t,x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\psi_{n}(t,x)

where
\psi_{n}
can be taken to be smooth functions supported on
[0,T+1]\times\big([n-1,n+1]\cup[-n-1,1-n]\big)
and
\|\psi_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}n^{2}}
, for some constant
\mathrm{C}>0
independent of
n
. Consequently, if we write
K^{-}=\sum_{n}K_{n}^{-}
where
K_{n}^{-}
corresponds to convolution with
\psi_{n}
, then the infinite sum of the operator norms
\sum_{n}\|K_{n}\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}\to C^{\alpha+2,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}}
may be shown to be finite using the fact that Gaussian decay of the
\psi_{n}
dominates the growth of the polynomial weights.

Finally, it remains to prove
K(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f=f
for
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})
with
\alpha<0
. To prove this, we remark that
Kf
may actually be defined for
any

f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with support contained in
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, via the formula

(Kf,\varphi):=(f,K^{\dagger}*\varphi),

where
K^{\dagger}(t,x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi|t|}}{e^{-x^{2}/2|t|}}\mathbf{1}_{\{t\leq 0\}}
and
*
denotes convolution in both space and time. The right side is well-defined because
f
is supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, and because one can ensure that if
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
then
K^{\dagger}*\varphi
agrees on that strip with an element of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
thanks to the Gaussian decay properties of
K^{\dagger}
in the spatial variable (this is our reason for working on
\Lambda_{T}
rather than all of
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
).

Then
Kf
as defined above will be an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
that is supported on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, and this definition of
Kf
agrees with the one in the proposition statement because it agrees on smooth test functions using integration by parts. Now we claim (more generally than stated in the proposition) that
K(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f=f
for
any

f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
that is supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
. But this just amounts to showing that
((\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f,K^{\dagger}*\varphi)=(f,\varphi)
for all such
f
and all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. By definition of
(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})f
, this is equivalent to showing
(f,(-\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})K^{\dagger}*\varphi)=(f,\varphi)
for all such
f
and all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. As
f
is supported on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
, this reduces to showing that
(-\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})K^{\dagger}*\varphi=\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
that are supported on
[-1,T+1]\times\mathbb{R}
(for instance), which is clear.
∎

Corollary 6.7
.

Define
J:C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
by

Jf(t,x):=\big(f,p_{t}(x-\cdot)\big)_{\mathbb{R}},

J
is a bounded linear operator for any
\alpha<1
and any
\tau>0.
Consider the operator
\widehat{P}_{t}:=JP_{t}
with
