P_{t}
defined in Proposition
6.5
. By the semigroup property of the heat kernel, we have that

(6.6)

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{s}f(t,x)=(Jf)(t+s,x).

Furthermore, the operators satisfy

(6.7)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{P}_{t}f\|_{C^{\beta,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot t^{-(\beta-\alpha)/2}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})},

where
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\alpha,\beta,T)>0
is independent of
t
and
f
.

Proof.

The first part follows by using the Schauder estimate in Proposition
6.6
, noting that
Jf=K(\delta_{0}\otimes f)
where for
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
the latter distribution is defined by
(\delta_{0}\otimes f,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}:=(f,\varphi(0,\cdot))_{\mathbb{R}}.
Directly from the definitions one can check that
f\mapsto\delta_{0}\otimes f
is bounded from
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T}).
Finally, (
6.7
) follows from Proposition
6.5
.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

Although we have only defined it for
0<\alpha<1
, one may actually define
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}
for all
\alpha>0
, as the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
with respect to

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})}:=\sup_{(t,x)\in\Lambda_{T}}\frac{|f(t,x)|}{w(x)}+\sup_{(t,x)\in\Lambda_{T}}\sup_{\lambda\in(0,1]}\sup_{\varphi\in B_{0}}\frac{(f,S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)}{w(x)\lambda^{\alpha}}

where the scaling operators are defined in (
6.4
) and where
B_{0}
is the set of all smooth functions of
C^{0}
norm less than 1, supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that are
orthogonal in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to all polynomials of parabolic degree less than or equal to

\lceil\alpha\rceil
, see for instance equation (3.8) and (3.9) in
[
CW17
]
. One may verify that this norm is equivalent to the one in Definition
6.3
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Stated in this way, the Schauder estimate actually holds for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Z},
though we will not need this.

We next define a space-time distribution that is supported on a single temporal cross-section:

Definition 6.9
.

Let
\alpha<0
. Given some
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
and
b\in[0,T]
we define
\delta_{b}\otimes f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
by the formula

(\delta_{b}\otimes f,\varphi)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}:=(f,\varphi(b,\cdot))_{\mathbb{R}}.

Directly from the definitions of the scaling operators in (
6.3
) and (
6.4
), it is clear that for fixed
b\in[0,T]
, the linear map
f\mapsto\delta_{b}\otimes f
is bounded from
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
as long as
\alpha<0.
It is also clear that
\delta_{b}\otimes f
is necessarily supported on the line
\{b\}\times\mathbb{R}
. The following lemma shows that under mild conditions,
\delta_{T}\otimes f
vanishes upon the action of the heat flow
K
defined in (
6.5
).

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}),
where
\alpha>-2.
Then viewing
K
as an operator from
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
, we have that

K(\delta_{T}\otimes f)=0.

Proof.

Note that
\delta_{T}\otimes f\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
where
\alpha>-4.
Thus by Proposition
6.6
, we know that
K(\delta_{T}\otimes f)\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\beta,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
for some
\beta>-2
. Now since
\delta_{T}\otimes f
is supported on
\{T\}\times\mathbb{R}
, it immediately follows that
K(\delta_{T}\otimes f)
is also supported on
\{T\}\times\mathbb{R}.

Thus it suffices to show that if
g\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\beta,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
for some
\beta\in(-2,0),
and if
g
is supported on
\{T\}\times\mathbb{R}
then
g=0.
We can prove this directly: fix some smooth nonnegative compactly supported function
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and define
g^{\lambda}(t,x):=(g,S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi),
where the scaling operators are given by (
6.4
). Using
g\in C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\beta,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
for some
\beta\in(-2,0),
one has that
g^{\lambda}(t,x)\leq Cw(x)|T-t|^{\frac{\beta}{2}}
where
C
is independent of
\lambda,x,t,
and where
w
is the weight function given by (
6.2
). Now if
\psi
is any compactly supported function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, then by integrability of
t\mapsto t^{\beta/2}
on
[0,1]
and the dominated convergence theorem, we find that

(g,\psi)=\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g^{\lambda}(t,x)\psi(t,x)dtdx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\lim_{\lambda\to 0}g^{\lambda}(t,x)\psi(t,x)dtdx=0,

where we used the fact that
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}g^{\lambda}(t,x)=0
at all points
(t,x)
outside of the measure-zero subset given by
\{T\}\times\mathbb{R}.

∎

We remark that the last paragraph of the above proof has an intuitive analogue in the elliptic setting as well: if
f\in C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
where
\alpha>-1
, and if
f
is supported on a single point, then
f=0
. The proof proceeds in a similar manner.

We end this subsection by recording a Kolmogorov-type lemma for the three spaces introduced at the beginning of this subsection. It will be crucial in proving tightness in those respective spaces.

Lemma 6.11

(Kolmogorov lemma)
.

Let
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be the space of all random variables defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with finite second moment. We have the following:

(a)

(Elliptic Hölder Space) Let
\phi\mapsto V(\phi)
be a bounded linear map from
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
into
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. Recall
S^{\lambda}_{x}
from (
6.3
). Assume there exists some
p>1
and
\alpha<0
and
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\alpha,p)>0
such that one has

\mathbf{E}[|V(S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|^{p}]\leq\mathrm{C}\lambda^{\alpha p},

uniformly over all smooth functions
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
, and uniformly over
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then for any
\tau>1
and any
\beta<\alpha-1/p
there exists a random variable
\mathscr{V}
taking values in
C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
such that
(\mathscr{V},\phi)=V(\phi)
almost surely for all
\phi.
Furthermore one has that

\mathbf{E}[\|\mathscr{V}\|^{p}_{C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}]\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime},

where
\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
depends on the choice of
\alpha,p,
and the constant
\mathrm{C}
appearing in the moment bound above but not on
V,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}
.

(b)

(Function space)
Let
(t,\phi)\mapsto V(t,\phi)
be a map from
[0,T]\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
into
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
which is linear and continuous in
\phi
. Fix a non-negative integer
r
. Assume there exists some
\kappa>0,p>1/\kappa
and
\alpha<-r
and
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\kappa,\alpha,p,{T})>0
such that one has

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[|V(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|^{p}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}\lambda^{\alpha p},

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[|V(t,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-V(s,S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|^{p}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}\lambda^{(\alpha-\kappa)p}|t-s|^{\kappa p},

uniformly over all smooth functions
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}
with
\|\phi\|_{C^{r}}\leq 1
, and uniformly over
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
0\leq s,t\leq T
. Then for any
\tau>1
and any
\beta<\alpha-\kappa
there exists a random variable
\big(\mathscr{V}(t)\big)_{t\in[0,T]}
taking values in
C([0,T],C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
such that
(\mathscr{V}(t),\phi)=V(t,\phi)
almost surely for all
\phi
and
t
. Furthermore, one has that

\mathbf{E}[\|\mathscr{V}\|^{p}_{C([0,T],C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}]\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime},

where
\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
depends on the choice of
\alpha,\beta,p,\kappa,
and the constant
\mathrm{C}
appearing in the moment bound above but not on
V,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}
.

(c)

(Parabolic Hölder Space) Let
\varphi\mapsto V(\varphi)
be a bounded linear map from
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. Assume
V(\varphi)=0
for all
\varphi
with support contained in the complement of
\Lambda_{T}
. Recall
S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}
from (
6.4
). Fix a non-negative integer
r
. Assume there exists some
p>1
and
\alpha<0
and
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(\alpha,p)>0
such that one has

\mathbf{E}[|V(S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)|^{p}]\leq\mathrm{C}\lambda^{\alpha p},

uniformly over all smooth functions
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\|\varphi\|_{C^{r}}\leq 1
, and uniformly over
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
(t,x)\in\Lambda_{T}
. Then for any
\tau>1
and any
\beta<\alpha-3/p
there exists a random variable
\mathscr{V}
taking values in
C^{\beta,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})
such that
(\mathscr{V},\varphi)=V(\varphi)
almost surely for all
\varphi.
Furthermore one has that

\mathbf{E}[\|\mathscr{V}\|^{p}_{C^{\beta,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})}]\leq\mathrm{C}^{\prime},

where
\mathrm{C}^{\prime}
depends on the choice of
\alpha,p,
and the constant
\mathrm{C}
appearing in the moment bound above but not on
V,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}
.

A proof of the above results may be adapted from the proof of Lemma 9 in Section 5 of
[
MW17
]
. We remark that we do not actually need uniformity over a large class of test functions as we have written above, just a single well-chosen test function would suffice (e.g. the Littlewood-Paley blocks as used in
[
MW17
]
or the Daubechies wavelets in
[
HL15
]
). Part (a) of the above lemma can be stated with
C^{r}
functions just like part (c), but we will not need this general version.

6.2.
Tightness

In this section, we prove tightness of the field
\mathscr{X}^{N}
and establish regularity estimates for the limit points. At this stage, readers may find it helpful to revisit Section
1.5.2
for a proof sketch of the tightness argument.

Proposition 6.12
.

For each
t\geq 0
, the fields
\{\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
defined in (
1.4
) may be realized as random elements of the elliptic Hölder space
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
for any
\tau>1
and any
\alpha<-1.
Moreover they are tight with respect to that topology as
N\to\infty
, in fact, we have that
\sup_{N}\mathbb{E}[\|\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}\|_{C^{-\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})}^{p}]<\infty
for all
p\geq 1
. Furthermore, the
\mathscr{X}^{N}
defined in (
1.6
) themselves may be viewed as elements of the parabolic Hölder space
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
for
\alpha<-1
and
\tau>1.
Moreover they are tight with respect to that topology as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
2.2
and Lemma
3.1
we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}(\phi)^{2k}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{D_{N^{1/4}}SB_{N^{1/2}\nu}^{(k)}}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{2k}\phi(X^{j}_{t})e^{{\frac{1}{2}}N^{1/2}\sum_{i<j}V_{t}^{ij}(\mathbf{X})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}_{SB_{N^{1/2}\nu}^{(k)}}\bigg[\mathcal{M}_{N}(t)\prod_{j=1}^{2k}\phi(X^{j}_{t})e^{{\frac{1}{2}}N^{1/2}\sum_{i<j}V_{t}^{ij}(\mathbf{X})}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi\|^{2k}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\mathbf{E}_{SB_{N^{1/2}\nu}^{(k)}}[\mathcal{M}_{N}(t)^{2}]^{1/2}\mathbf{E}_{SB_{N^{1/2}\nu}^{(k)}}\bigg[e^{N^{1/2}\sum_{i<j}V_{t}^{ij}(\mathbf{X})}\bigg]^{1/2},

where
\mathcal{M}_{N}(t)
is defined in (
2.15
) and the last inequality above is due to the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. From Remark
2.7
and Lemma
2.4
(with
\nu\mapsto N^{1/2}\nu
), we know that the above two expectations are uniformly bounded in
N
. Therefore the last expression is bounded by some universal constant times
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2k}.
In particular we get that

\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{X}_{t}^{N}(S_{x}^{\lambda}\phi)^{2k}]\leq\mathrm{C}\|S_{x}^{\lambda}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2k}\leq C\lambda^{-2k},

uniformly over all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1,
where
S_{x}^{\lambda}
is defined in (
6.3
). Appealing to Lemma
6.11

(a)
we get the desired result for fixed
t
.

To prove the second statement about tightness in the parabolic Hölder space, we will as in (
1.6
) denote space-time pairings as
(\mathscr{X}^{N},\varphi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
. Note by Minkowski’s inequality that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\mathscr{X}^{N},\varphi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2k}]^{1/2k}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg(\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\varphi(s,\cdot))ds\bigg)^{2k}\bigg]^{1/2k}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\varphi(s,\cdot))^{2k}]^{1/2k}ds.

Now with
S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}
defined in (
6.4
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\mathscr{X}^{N},S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2k}]^{1/2k}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}\big((S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}\varphi)(s,\cdot)\big)^{2k}\big]^{1/2k}ds\leq\mathrm{C}\int_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{t+\lambda^{2}}\lambda^{-3}ds\leq\mathrm{C}\lambda^{-1},

uniformly over all
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
supported on the unit ball, with
\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1.
Here the
\lambda^{-3}
comes from the fact that the parabolic scaling operator differs from the elliptic one by exactly a factor of
\lambda^{-2}
. In the second bound, we used the same bounds appearing in the proof for the elliptic case above, noting that the constants there are independent of
t\in[0,T]
. Appealing to Lemma
6.11

(c)
we get the desired result.
∎

Proposition 6.13
.

The fields
M^{N}
defined in (
4.4
) may be realized as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\alpha<-2
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology.
Q^{N}
defined in (
5.1
) may be realized as an element of
C([0,T],C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for any
\gamma<-1
and
\tau>1
. Moreover, they are tight with respect to that topology. Let
(M^{\infty},Q^{\infty})
be a joint limit point of
(M^{N},Q^{N})
in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})).
For all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the process
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))_{t\in[0,T]}
is a martingale with respect to the canonical filtration on that space, and moreover one has

(6.8)

\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2}).

We remark that the canonical filtration
\mathscr{F}_{t}
on
C([0,T],\Xi)
for any Polish space
\Xi
is the one generated by the random variables
X(s)
for
s\leq t
as
X
varies through all elements of
C([0,T],\Xi)
.

Proof.

Take any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1.
Recall
S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}
from (
6.4
). Using the first bound in Proposition
5.6
, we have

\mathbb{E}[(Q_{t}^{N}(S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-Q_{s}^{N}(S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi))^{k}]\leq\mathrm{C}|t-s|^{k/4}\lambda^{-k},

uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{R},\lambda\in(0,1],0\leq s,t\leq T
. Since
Q_{0}^{N}(\phi)=0
by definition, therefore the assumptions of Lemma
6.11

(b)
are satisfied for any
\kappa\leq 1/4
, any
p>1/\kappa
, and any
\alpha\leq-1
, and we conclude the desired tightness for
Q^{N}
.

Now we address the tightness of the
M^{N}
. By Lemma
4.3
and the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, we have that

\mathbb{E}[(M_{t}^{N}(\phi)-M_{s}^{N}(\phi))^{2k}]\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg((Q_{t}^{N}\big((\phi+N^{-1/4}\phi^{\prime})^{2}\big)-Q_{s}^{N}\big((\phi+N^{-1/4}\phi^{\prime})^{2}\big)\bigg)^{k}\bigg],

where
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(k)>0
is free of
\phi,s,t,N.
Using a crude bound

(\phi+N^{-1/4}\phi^{\prime})^{2}\leq 2\phi^{2}+2N^{-1/2}(\phi^{\prime})^{2}\leq 2\phi^{2}+2(\phi^{\prime})^{2},

we find from the first bound in Proposition
5.6
that

(6.9)

\mathbb{E}[(M_{t}^{N}(\phi)-M_{s}^{N}(\phi))^{2k}]\leq\mathrm{C}(t-s)^{k/4}(\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\phi^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2})^{k}\leq\mathrm{C}(t-s)^{k/4}\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}^{2k}.

This gives

\mathbb{E}[(M_{t}^{N}(S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)-M_{s}^{N}(S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi))^{2k}]\leq\mathrm{C}(t-s)^{k/4}\lambda^{-4k}

uniformly over
x\in\mathbb{R},\lambda\in(0,1],0\leq s,t\leq T
, and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\|\phi\|_{C^{1}}\leq 1.
Moreover
M_{0}(\phi)=0
by definition, therefore the assumptions of Lemma
6.11

(b)
are satisfied for any
\kappa\leq 1/8
, any
p>1/\kappa
, and any
\alpha\leq-2
. Hence, we conclude the desired tightness for
M^{N}
.

We next show that the limit point
M^{\infty}(\phi)
is a martingale. Since
M_{0}^{N}(\phi)=0
, from (
6.9
), we see that
\sup_{N}\mathbb{E}[M_{t}^{N}(\phi)^{2k}]<\infty.
Thus by uniform integrability,
M^{\infty}(\phi)
is a martingale. In the prelimit we know that

(M_{t}^{N}(\phi))^{2}-Q_{t}^{N}\big((\phi+N^{-1/4}\phi^{\prime})^{2}\big)=(M_{t}^{N}(\phi))^{2}-Q_{t}^{N}(\phi^{2})-\mathcal{E}_{t}^{N}(\phi)

is a martingale, where the error term
\mathcal{E}_{t}^{N}
is defined in (
5.2
). By Proposition
5.5
we know that
\mathcal{E}_{t}^{N}
vanishes in probability, so we conclude (again by uniform
L^{p}
boundedness guaranteed by Proposition
5.6
) that
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))^{2}-Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2})

is a martingale. This verifies (
6.8
) completing the proof.
∎

Proposition 6.14
.

For
\alpha<0
, the derivative operator
\partial_{s}:C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
which is defined by sending
(v_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
to the distribution

(\partial_{s}v,\varphi)_{L^{2}(\Lambda_{T})}:=v_{T}(\varphi(T,\cdot))-\int_{0}^{T}v_{t}(\partial_{t}\varphi(t,\cdot))dt-v_{0}(\varphi(0,\cdot)),

whenever
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T}),
is a bounded linear map. Let
M^{N}
be as in Proposition
6.13
, viewed as elements of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for some
\alpha<-2.
Also let
\mathscr{X}^{N}_{t}
and
\mathscr{X}^{N}
and be as in Proposition
6.12
, viewed as elements of
C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
and
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\gamma,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
respectively for some
\gamma<-1
. Then one has that

(6.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}M^{N}=-\delta_{(0,0)}+(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})\mathscr{X}^{N}+\delta_{T}\otimes\mathscr{X}^{N}_{T}

in the sense of distributions.

Proof.

Recall
S^{\lambda}_{(t,x)}
from (
6.4
). From the definition of the space
C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
one verifies directly that if
v=(v_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
then

\int_{0}^{T}|v_{t}\big(\partial_{t}(S^{\lambda}_{(s,y)}\varphi)(t,\cdot)\big)|dt\leq w(y)\int_{s-\lambda^{2}}^{s+\lambda^{2}}\lambda^{\alpha-4}dt\leq w(y)\lambda^{\alpha-2}

uniformly over
(s,y)\in\Lambda_{T}
and
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\|\varphi\|_{C^{r}}\leq 1
where
r=\lceil-\alpha\rceil.
This proves the first part of the proposition that
\partial_{s}
is bounded from
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T}).

For the second part, let us abbreviate
\mathscr{M}^{N}:=\partial_{s}M^{N}
. We first note by definition of
\partial_{s}
that for any smooth
\varphi
of compact support on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\mathscr{M}^{N}(\varphi)
is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}^{N}(\varphi)=M_{T}^{N}(\varphi(T,\cdot))-\int_{0}^{T}\hskip-2.84544ptM^{N}_{s}\big(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)ds,

where we used the fact that
M_{0}^{N}\equiv 0
. Note that the above expression is the same as
R_{T}^{N}(\varphi)
defined in (
4.5
). Now observe that using (
4.4
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}M^{N}_{s}\big(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\hskip-5.69046pt\mathscr{X}^{N}_{s}\big(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)ds-\int_{0}^{T}\big(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,0)\big)ds

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{s}\hskip-5.69046pt\mathscr{X}_{u}^{N}\big(\partial_{xx}\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)duds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\mathscr{X}^{N}_{s}\big(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)ds+\varphi(0,0)-\varphi(T,0)

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{s}\mathscr{X}_{u}^{N}\big(\partial_{xx}\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)duds.

The last term on the right-hand side of the above equation can be simplified as follows.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{s}\mathscr{X}_{u}^{N}\big(\partial_{xx}\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)duds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\int_{u}^{T}\mathscr{X}_{u}^{N}\big(\partial_{xx}\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)dsdu

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\big[\mathscr{X}_{u}^{N}\big(\partial_{xx}\varphi(T,\cdot)\big)-\mathscr{X}_{u}^{N}\big(\partial_{xx}\varphi(u,\cdot)\big)\big]du

\displaystyle=-M_{T}^{N}(\varphi(T,\cdot))+\mathscr{X}_{T}^{N}(\varphi(T,\cdot))-\varphi(T,0)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathscr{X}_{u}^{N}(\partial_{xx}\varphi(u,\cdot)\big)du.

We applied (
4.4
) with
\phi=\varphi(T,\cdot)
in the last line. Combining these expressions, we derive that

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}^{N}(\varphi)=-\varphi(0,0)-\int_{0}^{T}\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,\cdot)ds-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathscr{X}_{s}^{N}(\partial_{xx}\varphi(s,\cdot)\big)ds+\mathscr{X}_{T}^{N}(\varphi(T,\cdot)).

This verifies (
6.10
) completing the proof.
∎

Since we expect to obtain a Dirac initial data in the limiting SPDE, there is an additional singularity at the origin that we have not yet taken into account. To fix this issue, we now formulate a tightness result taking into account this singularity, by starting our martingale-forced heat equation from some positive time
\varepsilon
(which should be thought of as being close to
0
).

Proposition 6.15

(Tightness)
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0,
and consider the fields
\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon}(t,\cdot):=\mathscr{X}_{t+\varepsilon}^{N}(\cdot),
for
t\geq 0
. Set
\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon}(t,\cdot):=0
for
t<0
. The fields
\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon}
may be realized as random variables taking values in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
for any
\tau>1
and
\alpha<-1
. Furthermore, they are tight with respect to that topology, and any limit point necessarily lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
for any
\kappa>0
.

Proof.

The proof that the fields
\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon}
are tight in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
for any
\tau>1
and
\alpha<-1
follows by using the exact same strategy followed in the proof of Proposition
6.12
. Let us set

\mathscr{M}^{N,\varepsilon}:=\big(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\big)\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon}+(\delta_{T}\otimes\mathscr{X}^{N}_{T+\varepsilon}-\delta_{0}\otimes\mathscr{X}^{N}_{\varepsilon}).

We first claim that for fixed
\varepsilon>0
, the fields
\{\mathscr{M}^{N,\varepsilon}\}_{N>1}
are tight in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-3-\kappa,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
for any
\tau>1
and
\kappa>0
. As
\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon}
is tight in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-1-\kappa,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
, the tightness of the
(\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon}
in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-3-\kappa,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
is immediate since
\partial_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}
is a bounded linear map from
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
into
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha-2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
as explained in Remark
6.4
. From Proposition
6.12
we know that both
\mathscr{X}^{N}_{T+\varepsilon}
and
\mathscr{X}^{N}_{\varepsilon}
are tight in
C^{-1-\kappa,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
. As we remarked after Definition
6.9
, the map
f\mapsto\delta_{a}\otimes f
is continuous from
C^{-1-\kappa,\tau}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-3-\kappa,\tau}(\Lambda_{T}),
therefore we can immediately conclude that
(\delta_{T}\otimes\mathscr{X}^{N}_{T+\varepsilon}-\delta_{0}\otimes\mathscr{X}^{N}_{\varepsilon})
are tight in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-3-\kappa,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
. Hence, the sum given by
\mathscr{M}^{N,\varepsilon}
is also tight in the latter space as
N\to\infty
(for fixed
\varepsilon
).

Next, we address the regularity of the limit points of
\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon}.
By the last statement in Proposition
6.6
, we can apply the linear operator
K
to both sides to obtain

\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon}=K(\delta_{0}\otimes\mathscr{X}^{N}_{\varepsilon})+K\mathscr{M}^{N,\varepsilon}-K(\delta_{T}\otimes\mathscr{X}^{N}_{T+\varepsilon}).

By Proposition
6.12
we know that
\mathscr{X}^{N}_{T+\varepsilon}
is tight in
C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
for
\gamma<-1
, and from Lemma
6.10
we can conclude that
K(\delta_{T}\otimes\mathscr{X}^{N}_{T+\varepsilon})=0
. On the other hand,
K(\delta_{0}\otimes\mathscr{X}^{N}_{\varepsilon})=J\mathscr{X}^{N}_{\varepsilon}
, where
J
is defined in Corollary
6.7
. We thus have the Duhamel equation

\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon}=J\mathscr{X}^{N}_{\varepsilon}+K\mathscr{M}^{N,\varepsilon}.

For technical reasons that will be made clear below, we now replace
\varepsilon
by
\varepsilon/2
and
T
by
T+1
. If we take any joint limit point
(\mathscr{X}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2},\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2},h)
in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T+1})\times C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha+2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T+1})\times C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})
as
N\to\infty
of
(\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon/2},\mathscr{M}^{N,\varepsilon/2},\mathscr{X}^{N}_{\varepsilon/2})
, then (by continuity of
J
and
K
on the relevant spaces) the Duhamel relation still holds in the limit, i.e.,

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}=Jh+K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}.

We now claim that

(6.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}\|_{C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa-3/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T+1})}^{p}]<\infty.

Let us now complete the proof of regularity assuming (
6.12
).

Using (
6.12
) and Proposition
6.6
, it follows that

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big\|K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}\big\|_{C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T+1})}^{p}\bigg]<\infty.

This implies that the restriction of
K\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}
to
[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]\times\mathbb{R}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]})
.

•

By Proposition
6.12
we have
\mathbb{E}[\|h\|_{C^{-1-\kappa,\tau}}^{p}]<\infty
. So from (
6.7
) with
\alpha:=-1-\kappa
and
\beta:=\frac{1}{2}-\kappa
. It follows that

\mathbb{E}[\|\widehat{P}_{\varepsilon/2}h\|_{C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T+1})}^{p}]<\infty.

Since from (
6.6
), we know
Jh(t+\varepsilon/2,x)=\widehat{P}_{\varepsilon/2}h(t,x)
, we thus have that the restriction of
Jh
to
[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]\times\mathbb{R}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]})
.

Thanks to the above two bullet points and the relation (
6.11
), we have showed that the restriction of
\mathscr{X}^{\infty,\varepsilon/2}
to
[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]\times\mathbb{R}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{[\varepsilon/2,T+\varepsilon/2]})
. This is equivalent to the fact that
\mathscr{X}^{\infty,\varepsilon}
lies in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
. This completes the proof modulo (
6.12
).

Proof of (
6.12
)
. We shall show (
6.12
) with
\varepsilon/2
and
T+1
replaced by
\varepsilon
and
T
respectively. Following the same proof as in Lemma
6.14
, one can check that

\mathscr{M}^{N,\varepsilon}(\varphi)=R^{N}_{T+\varepsilon}(\varphi)-R^{N}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi),

where
R^{N}
are the same martingales defined in (
4.5
). We will denote the right side as
R^{N,\varepsilon}(\varphi)
and the quadratic variation up to time
T
as
\langle R^{N,\varepsilon}(\varphi)\rangle_{T}.
Let us fix any
\alpha<-3
and take any joint limit point
(\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon},\mathscr{X}^{\infty,\varepsilon})
in
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})\times C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha+2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
of
(\mathscr{M}^{N,\varepsilon},\mathscr{X}^{N,\varepsilon})
as
N\to\infty
. Let us consider any smooth function
\varphi
supported on the unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\|\varphi\|_{C^{1}}\leq 1.
Recall
S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}
from (
6.4
). Using Skorohod’s lemma, Fatou’s lemma, and the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, one has that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)|^{p}]

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[|\mathscr{M}^{N,\varepsilon}(\varphi)|^{p}]\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\langle R^{N,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)\rangle_{T}^{p/2}\big],

where
\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{C}(p)>0
. From Lemma
4.4
, the quadratic variation is given by

\displaystyle\langle R^{N,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)\rangle_{T}=N^{1/2}\int_{0}^{T}\big((\mathscr{X}_{s+\varepsilon}^{N})^{Sq},((1+N^{-1/4}\partial_{x})S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi(s,\cdot))^{2}\big)ds.

Using
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2}),
it is clear that
\big((1+N^{-1/4}\partial_{x})\varphi\big)^{2}\leq 2\varphi^{2}+2N^{-1/2}(\partial_{x}\varphi)^{2}
. Furthermore, the term with a factor
N^{-1/2}
will not contribute when taking the
\liminf
as
N\to\infty
(by e.g. Proposition
5.5
).
Therefore, we can obtain that

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\langle R^{N,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)\rangle_{T}]\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}N^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\big((\mathscr{X}_{s+\varepsilon}^{N})^{Sq},(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)^{2}\big)ds\bigg].

Recall the fields
Q^{N}
defined in (
5.1
). Notice that
\big(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi\big)^{2}\leq\lambda^{-6}\mathbf{1}_{[t-\lambda^{2},t+\lambda^{2}]\times[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]}
. Therefore, taking any
\delta>0
, by the above bound and by the second bound in Proposition
5.6
we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\mathscr{M}^{\infty,\varepsilon}(S_{(t,x)}^{\lambda}\varphi)|^{p}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathrm{C}\lambda^{-3p}\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\big(Q^{N}_{t+\varepsilon+\lambda^{2}}(\mathbf{1}_{[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]})-Q^{N}_{t+\varepsilon-\lambda^{2}}(\mathbf{1}_{[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]})\big)^{p/2}\big]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathrm{C}\lambda^{-3p}(\lambda^{2})^{p/2}\|\mathbf{1}_{[x-\lambda,x+\lambda]}\|_{L^{1+\delta}(\mathbb{R})}^{p/2}=\mathrm{C}\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}p-\big[\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}\big]p}.

Given any
\kappa>0
, by making
\delta=\delta(\kappa)
close to
0
and making
p=p(\kappa)
large enough, we may then apply Lemma
6.11
(c)
to conclude (
6.12
).
∎

6.3.
Identification of the limit points

In this subsection, we identify the limit points as the solution of the stochastic heat equation (
SHE
) and thereby prove our weak convergence theorem: Theorem
1.4
.

Theorem 6.16

(Solving the martingale problem)
.

Consider the triplet of processes
(\mathscr{X}^{N},M^{N},Q^{N})_{t\geq 0}
, where
\mathscr{X}^{N}
,
M^{N}
, and
Q^{N}
are defined in (
1.4
), (
4.4
), and (
5.1
) respectively. Fix
\alpha<-1,\beta<-2,\gamma<-1,
and
\tau>1.
These triples are jointly tight in the space

C^{\alpha,\tau}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\Lambda_{T})\times C([0,T],C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\times C([0,T],C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R})).

Consider any joint limit point
(X^{\infty},M^{\infty},Q^{\infty})
. Then for any
s>0
, the process
(t,x)\mapsto X_{s+t}^{\infty}(x)
is necessarily supported on the space
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
. Furthermore,
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))_{t\geq 0}
is a continuous martingale for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, and moreover for all
0<s\leq t<T
one has the almost sure identities

(6.13)

\displaystyle M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)-M_{s}^{\infty}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(X_{t}^{\infty}(x)-X_{s}^{\infty}(x))\phi(x)dx-\frac{1}{2}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}X_{u}^{\infty}(x)\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)dxdu

(6.14)

\displaystyle\langle M^{\infty}(\phi)\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi^{2})

(6.15)

\displaystyle Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)-Q_{s}^{\infty}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{s}^{t}(X_{u}^{\infty}(x))^{2}\phi(x)\,du\,dx.

Before going into the proof, we remark that with some inspection it may be verified that all quantities make sense given the spaces they lie in, as long as we choose
\kappa<1/2
to ensure that
X^{\infty}
is a continuous function in space-time away from
t=0
.

Proof.

Most parts of the following theorem are already established in previous propositions and lemmas. Note that the tightness of
\mathscr{X}^{N}
was proved in Proposition
6.12
. The tightness of
M^{N}
and
Q^{N}
was shown in Proposition
6.13
. Thus the joint tightness follows from individual tightness. Consider any limit point
(\mathscr{X}^{\infty},M^{\infty},Q^{\infty}).
The fact that for any
s>0
, the process
(t,x)\mapsto X^{\infty}(s+t,x)
is necessarily supported on the space
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
was proved in Proposition
6.15
. From Proposition
6.13
we have that for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
the process
(M_{t}^{\infty}(\phi))_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale. (
6.14
) is already proven in Proposition
6.13
as (
6.8
). All we are left to show is (
6.13
) and (
6.15
).

Proof of (
6.13
).

In Lemma
6.14
we obtained that
\partial_{s}M^{N}=-\delta_{(0,0)}+(\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})\mathscr{X}^{N}+\delta_{T}\otimes\mathscr{X}^{N}_{T}
in the sense of distributions, which by disregarding the boundary terms implies that

(\partial_{s}M^{N},\varphi)=((\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})\mathscr{X}^{N},\varphi)

for all
\varphi
of compact support contained in
\Lambda_{[\varepsilon,T-\varepsilon]}
for some
\varepsilon>0
. Note that this relation is still respected by any limit points and hence remains true when
N=\infty
. We thus have

(\partial_{s}M^{\infty},\varphi)=((\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2})\mathscr{X}^{\infty},\varphi)

Since both
\mathscr{X}^{\infty}
and
M^{\infty}
lie in spaces with strong enough topologies (
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{-\kappa+1/2,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
and
C([0,T],C^{\beta,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
respectively), taking
\varphi(u,x)
to approach the function
(u,x)\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{[s,t]}(u)\phi(x)
in the above equation leads to (
6.13
).

Proof of (
6.15
).
Fix any
0\leq t\leq T
and let
\mu
be a
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
valued random variable defined as

\displaystyle(\mu,\phi):=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\phi)-\sigma\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{t}(\mathscr{X}_{s}^{\infty}(x))^{2}\phi(x)\,ds\,dx.

We claim that
(\mu,\phi)=0
a.s. for all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}).
This will validate (
6.15
). To verify this, let us define a sequence of function-valued random variables:

\displaystyle\mu^{\varepsilon}(a):=Q_{t}^{\infty}(\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a})-\sigma\int_{0}^{t}(\mathscr{X}_{s}^{\infty}(\xi_{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}^{a}))^{2}ds

where
\xi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(x):=\varepsilon^{-1}\xi(\varepsilon^{-1}(x-a))
with
\xi(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x^{2}}
. Observe that for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
supported on
[-S,S]
,
by Cauchy-Schwarz and Jensen’s inequality we have that

\mathbb{E}\big[|(\mu^{\varepsilon},\phi)|\big]\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sqrt{\int_{[-S,S]}\mu^{\varepsilon}(a)^{2}da}\bigg]\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}\bigg[\int_{[-S,S]}\mathbb{E}[\mu^{\varepsilon}(a)^{2}]da\bigg]^{1/2}.

We now make use of Proposition
5.3
. From (
5.9
), we see that the integral is uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon>0
. On the other hand, (
5.8
) tells us that the integrand goes to zero as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Thus, by the dominated convergence theorem
(\mu^{\varepsilon},\phi)\to 0
in probability. But
(\mu^{\varepsilon},\phi)\to(\mu,\phi)
almost surely as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Hence,
(\mu,\phi)=0
almost surely. This proves the claim for compactly supported
\phi
. For general
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, we may find a sequence of functions
\phi_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\phi_{n}\to\phi
in the topology of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
0=(\mu,\phi_{n})\to(\mu,\phi)
almost surely as
n\to\infty
. Hence,
(\mu,\phi)=0
almost surely for all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. This verifies the claim, completing the proof.
∎

We now complete the proof of our main theorem, Theorem
1.4
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
(a)
.

We continue with the notation and setup of Theorem
6.16
. We have already established the tightness of
\mathscr{X}^{N}
in the topology of
C_{\mathfrak{s}}^{\alpha,\tau}(\Lambda_{T})
in Proposition
6.12
. Consider any limit point
X^{\infty}
of
\mathscr{X}^{N}
. From the previous theorem, we already know that
(t,x)\mapsto X_{t+\varepsilon}^{\infty}(x)
is a continuous function in space and time. From the three equations (
6.13
), (
6.14
), and (
6.15
) in Theorem
6.16
it follows that the martingale problem for (
SHE
) is satisfied by any limit point
X^{\infty}
. We refer the reader to
[
BG97
, Proposition 4.11]
for the characterization of the law of (
SHE
) as the solution to this martingale problem.

The result there is only stated for continuous initial conditions, so what this really shows is that for any
\varepsilon>0
the law of the continuous field
(t,x)\mapsto X_{t+\varepsilon}^{\infty}(x)
is that of the solution of (
SHE
) with initial condition
X_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}(\cdot).
Thus we still need to pin down the initial data as
\delta_{0}
, by showing that we can let
\varepsilon\to 0
and see that the limit of
X_{\varepsilon}^{\infty}(\cdot)
is equal to
\delta_{0}
in some sense.

In
[
Par18
, Section 6]
there is a general approach to do exactly this. Specifically, it suffices to show as in Lemma 6.6 of that paper the two bounds

(6.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|X_{t}^{\infty}(x)|^{r}]^{2/r}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot t^{-1/2}p_{t}(x),

(6.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|X_{t}^{\infty}(x)-p_{t}(x)|^{r}]^{2/r}\leq\mathrm{C}\cdot p_{t}(x),

where
r>1
is arbitrary,
p_{t}
is the standard heat kernel, and
\mathrm{C}
is independent of
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Clearly, it suffices to show this when
r
is an even integer.
Using
L^{2}
bounds for each term in the chaos expansion of (
SHE
) with
\delta_{0}
initial condition (see Lemma 2.4 in
[
Cor18
]
), it follows that the solution of (
SHE
) with
\delta_{0}
initial condition satisfies the above two bounds for
r=2
. The bounds for general
r
then follow from the
r=2
case by the hypercontractivity inequality
[
Nua06
, Theorem 1.4.1]
. By the moment convergence result (Proposition
1.3
), we know any limit point
X^{\infty}
must satisfy
\mathbb{E}[(\int_{\mathbb{R}}X_{t}^{\infty}(x)\phi(x)dx)^{k}]=\mathbf{E}[(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Z}_{t}(x)\phi(x)dx)^{k}]
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, where
(t,x)\mapsto\mathcal{Z}_{t}(x)
solves (
SHE
) with
\delta_{0}
initial data and
\sigma=\frac{1}{{2}\nu([0,1])}
. From here we can conclude by letting
\phi\to\delta_{x}
that
\mathbb{E}[X_{t}^{\infty}(x)^{k}]=\mathbf{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{t}(x)^{k}]
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Consequently, we may immediately deduce (
6.16
) and (
6.17
) by the corresponding bounds for
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Finally, we conclude this section by proving Theorem
1.4
(b)
which follows from the existence of a certain bounded linear operator.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
(b)
.

Fix
\alpha<0
. We claim that there exists a bounded linear map
\overline{K\partial_{s}}:C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\to C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
such that for all
v=(v_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\in C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
one has that
\overline{K\partial_{s}}v=K(\partial_{s}(v))
where
K
and
\partial_{s}
are the same operators defined in Propositions
6.6
and
6.14
respectively (where the equality may be interpreted in the sense that both sides are viewed as elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
).

Note that the existence of
\overline{K\partial_{s}}
would immediately imply the desired result. Indeed by applying
K
to both sides of (
6.10
) and using Proposition
6.6
and Lemma
6.10
we get that

\mathscr{X}^{N}=p+K(\partial_{s}(M^{N})),

where
p
is the standard heat kernel. We also know from Proposition
6.13
that the
M^{N}
are tight in
C([0,T],C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for
\gamma<-2
, therefore tightness of
\mathscr{X}^{N}
in
C([0,T],C^{\gamma,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
for
\gamma<-2
would be immediate by continuity of
\overline{K\partial_{s}}
. The fact that any limit point coincides with the law of (
SHE
) follows from Theorem
6.16
, (
6.16
) and (
6.17
) by taking a joint limit point with the triple appearing there, then using the fact that
K(\partial_{s}(M^{N}))=\overline{K\partial_{s}}M^{N}
.

We now turn toward the existence of
\overline{K\partial_{s}}
. To construct
\overline{K\partial_{s}}
we first remark that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
embeds into
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
in the natural way: a function
v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
may be identified with
(v(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
given by
(v(t),\phi)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}v(t,x)\phi(x)dx.
Then
\partial_{s}v=:v^{\prime}
is given by
(v^{\prime}(t),\phi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial_{t}v)(t,x)\phi(x)dx.
In this case we may integrate by parts to obtain

\displaystyle(K(\partial_{s}v)(t),\phi)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\int_{0}^{t}P_{t-s}v^{\prime}(s)ds\;,\;\phi\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg(v(t)-P_{t}v(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}P_{t-s}v(s)ds\;,\;\phi\bigg)

\displaystyle=(v(t),\phi)-(v(0),P_{t}\phi)-\int_{0}^{t}(v(s),P_{t-s}\partial_{x}^{2}\phi)ds,

where all pairings are in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and we use that
\partial_{s}P_{t-s}=-\partial_{x}^{2}P_{t-s},
and
\partial_{x}^{2}
and
P_{t}
are self-adjoint operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Based on this calculation, we consider a general path
v\in C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
and we finally define
\overline{K\partial_{s}}v
suggestively by the formula

(6.18)

\big(\big[\;\overline{K\partial_{s}}v\;\big](t),\phi):=(v(t),\phi)-(v(0),P_{t}\phi)-\int_{0}^{t}(v(s),P_{t-s}\partial_{x}^{2}\phi)ds.

First note that the pairings on the right-hand side are indeed well-defined for all
v\in C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
. What remains to be seen is that the integral is convergent and that the right-hand side is indeed an element of
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
whose norm may be controlled by
\|v\|_{C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))}.
By the definition of these spaces, we must replace
\phi
by
S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi
(where the scaling operators are given in Definition
6.1
) and then study the growth as
\lambda
becomes close to 0.

The first two terms on the right side of (
6.18
) are completely straightforward to deal with: the growth is at worst
\lambda^{\alpha}(1+x^{2})^{\tau}
uniformly over
\lambda,\phi,x,T
, since we assumed
v\in C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})).
To deal with the third term, we claim that one has

(6.19)

|(v(s),P_{t-s}\partial_{x}^{2}S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi)|\lesssim\big(\lambda^{\alpha-2}\wedge(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\big)(1+x^{2})^{\tau}

uniformly over
s<t\in[0,T]
, as well as
\lambda,\phi,x
. Indeed the bound of the form
\lambda^{\alpha-2}
follows by noting that when
t-s
is much smaller than
\lambda
,
P_{t-s}
is essentially the identity operator, so we can effectively disregard the heat kernel and note that
\partial_{x}^{2}S^{\lambda}_{x}\phi
paired with
v(s)
satisfies a bound of order
\lambda^{\alpha-2}
. Likewise the bound of the form
(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}
is obtained by noting that when
\lambda
is very small compared to
t-s
,
P_{t-s}S_{x}^{\lambda}\phi
behaves like
S_{x}^{\sqrt{t-s}}\phi
, giving a bound of order
(\sqrt{t-s})^{\alpha-2}
after applying
\partial_{x}^{2}
and pairing with
v(s)
. This proves the bound (
6.19
).

Now the fact that
\|\overline{K\partial_{s}}v\|
can be controlled by
\|v\|
follows simply by noting that uniformly over
0<\lambda^{2}\leq t\leq T
one has

\int_{0}^{t}\big(\lambda^{\alpha-2}\wedge(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\big)ds=\int_{0}^{t-\lambda^{2}}(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}ds+\int_{t-\lambda^{2}}^{t}\lambda^{\alpha-2}ds\leq\mathrm{C}(\alpha)\cdot\lambda^{\alpha}.

It remains to verify that
\overline{K\partial_{s}}v
is indeed a
continuous
path. So far, what we have effectively shown is that
\overline{K\partial_{s}}
is a bounded operator from
L^{\infty}([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
to itself. Note that
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
is precisely the closure of
\mathcal{S}(\Lambda_{T})
inside of
L^{\infty}([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})),
where
\mathcal{S}(\Lambda_{T})
denotes restrictions to
\Lambda_{T}
of those functions in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. From here, path continuity of
\overline{K\partial_{s}}v
for all
v\in C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
is immediate because the bounded operator
\overline{K\partial_{s}}:L^{\infty}([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))\to L^{\infty}([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R}))
maps the linear subspace
\mathcal{S}(\Lambda_{T})
into itself.

Since
\overline{K\partial_{s}}
is indeed a bounded operator, and since
\overline{K\partial_{s}}v=K(\partial_{s}(v))
for smooth paths
v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
as shown above, this equality remains true for general
v
by the denseness of the linear subspace
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda_{T})
in
C([0,T],C^{\alpha,\tau}(\mathbb{R})).

∎

We remark that the above proof is essentially sharp, in the sense that
K
improves parabolic regularity by
2
, while
\partial_{s}
destroys parabolic regularity by
2
. Hence, we expect their composition to preserve regularity, which is what we showed. Nonetheless, the Hölder exponent appearing in Theorem
1.4
(b)
may not be sharp, because we do not know if we obtained tightness of
M^{N}
in the best possible space in Proposition
6.13
.

7.
Quenched tail field

The goal of this section is to prove the results from Section
1.3.1
, namely Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.8
. Recall the quenched tail field
F_{N}(t,x)
from Definition
1.6
. We begin with a preliminary estimate for the first moment of
F_{N}(t,x)
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
B
be a standard Brownian motion. For all
t>0,x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x)]=N^{1/4}\mathbf{E}[e^{-N^{1/4}(B_{t}-x)}\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{t}\geq x\}}]\leq(2\pi t)^{-1/2}.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by writing
F_{N}
as a quenched expectation, exactly as we did in Section
1.5.1
or Section
2
. Then one takes the annealed expectation over the quenched expectation and uses the scale invariance of Brownian motion to obtain

\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x)]=N^{1/4}e^{\frac{t}{2}\sqrt{N}+N^{1/4}x}\mathbf{P}(B_{t}-N^{1/4}t\geq x).

Now applying Girsanov with the stochastic exponential of
-N^{1/4}B
will give the claimed equality. To prove the inequality, we note that

\displaystyle N^{1/4}\mathbf{E}[e^{-N^{1/4}(B_{t}-x)}\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{t}\geq x\}}]

\displaystyle=N^{1/4}\int_{x}^{\infty}e^{-N^{1/4}(u-x)}p_{t}(u)du

\displaystyle\leq(2\pi t)^{-1/2}\int_{x}^{\infty}N^{1/4}e^{-N^{1/4}(u-x)}du=(2\pi t)^{-1/2}.

∎

We first prove a variant of Theorem
1.7
where the quenched tail field is integrated against test functions.

Proposition 7.2
.

Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{m}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}>0
we have

(7.1)

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}(x)F_{N}(t_{i},x)dx\bigg)_{i=1}^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}(x)\mathcal{Z}_{t_{i}}(x)dx\bigg)_{i=1}^{m}.

Proof.

Let us formally define

\mu_{t}^{N}(dx):=N^{1/2}e^{\frac{t}{2}\sqrt{N}}K_{0,Nt}(N^{3/4}t+N^{1/2}x)dx.

The above definition can be interpreted rigorously as a random Borel measure by pushing forward
K_{0,Nt}
under a linear change of coordinates, just as we did in (
1.4
). Then we notice that Definition
1.6
is equivalent to

F_{N}(t,x):=N^{1/4}e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{t}^{N}[x,\infty).

Integrating by parts we have

\int_{-\infty}^{x}N^{1/4}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{t}^{N}[u,\infty)du=\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{t}^{N}(du)+e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{t}^{N}[x,\infty).

In the sense of distributions, it is clear that

\displaystyle F_{N}(t,x)=\partial_{x}\bigg[\int_{-\infty}^{x}N^{1/4}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{t}^{N}[u,\infty)du\bigg],\quad e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{t}^{N}(dx)=\partial_{x}\bigg[\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{t}^{N}(du)\bigg].

Consequently, for each
1\leq i\leq m
, we can repeatedly integrate by parts to obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}(x)F_{N}(t_{i},x)dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)\bigg[\int_{-\infty}^{x}N^{1/4}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{t_{i}}^{N}[u,\infty)du\bigg]dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)\bigg[\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{N^{1/4}u}\mu_{t_{i}}^{N}(du)+e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{t_{i}}^{N}[x,\infty)\bigg]dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}(x)e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{t_{i}}^{N}(dx)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{t_{i}}^{N}[x,\infty)dx.

Thanks to Theorem
1.4
(b)
(see Remark
1.5
(b)
) we know that as
N\to\infty

\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}(x)e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{t_{i}}^{N}(dx)\bigg)_{i=1}^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}(x)\mathcal{Z}_{t_{i}}(x)dx\bigg)_{i=1}^{m}.

Thus, to show (
7.1
) it suffices to show that as
N\to\infty
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{t_{i}}^{N}[x,\infty)dx
goes to zero in
L^{1}(\mathbb{P})
for each
1\leq i\leq m
. Indeed, observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)e^{N^{1/4}x}\mu_{t_{i}}^{N}[x,\infty)dx\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x)|e^{N^{1/4}x}\mathbb{E}[\mu_{t_{i}}^{N}[x,\infty)]dx.

By Lemma
7.1
we get

e^{N^{1/4}x}\mathbb{E}[\mu_{t_{i}}^{N}[x,\infty)]=\mathbf{E}[e^{-N^{1/4}(B_{t_{i}}-x)}\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{t_{i}}\geq x\}}]\leq N^{-1/4}(2\pi t_{i})^{-1/2}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, which implies the desired
L^{1}
convergence. This completes the proof.
∎

Note that composition with the logarithm function is not a continuous operation in the topology of integration against test functions, hence the convergence result stated above cannot be directly translated to any convergence result for the KPZ equation.
Our next lemma demonstrates uniform convergence for the two-point correlation function of the quenched tail field. This is the crucial result that allows us to improve Proposition
7.2
to obtain multipoint convergence in law of
F_{N}(t,x)
to
\mathcal{Z}_{t}(x)
for
individual
values of
(t,x)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, after which taking a logarithm becomes sensible.

Lemma 7.3
.

As
N\to\infty
, we have that

\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x)F_{N}(t,y)]\to\mathbf{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{t}(x)\mathcal{Z}_{t}(y)]

where
\mathcal{Z}
as in Proposition
1.3
. Furthermore, the above convergence is uniform over compact sets of
(t,x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

By adapting the methods in Section
2
, the two-point correlation function given by
(t,x,y)\mapsto\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x)F_{N}(t,y)]
can be written as an expression involving only the 2-point motion associated to the kernels
K_{0,t}
. More specifically, in the notation of Section
2
, it equals

Ne^{t\sqrt{N}}\mathbf{P}_{SB^{(2)}_{N^{1/2}\nu}}(X_{t}-N^{1/4}t\geq x,Y_{t}-N^{1/4}t\geq y).

Consequently the two-point correlation function only depends on the total mass of the characteristic measure. We shall thus assume
\nu
is a multiple of uniform measure. Without loss of generality, we will assume
\sigma=1
(i.e.,
\nu
is precisely half times the uniform measure).

We shall now invoke results from
[
BR20
]
which are for the case where the characteristic measure is a multiple of the uniform measure on
[0,1]
. In
[
BR20
]
, the authors consider a slightly different field
\widehat{F}_{N}(t,x)
which is “dual” to ours in a certain sense. More specifically, they vary the starting point
x
and fix the tail probability as
[0,\infty)
, whereas we fix the starting point
0
and vary the tail probability as
[x,\infty)
. One may show that the distributions of both fields are the same as space-time processes, modulo a reflection of the characteristic measure. More precisely, we have
F_{N}^{\nu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\widehat{F}_{N}^{\mu}
, where
\mu
is the pushforward of
\nu
by
x\mapsto 1-x
, and the superscript highlights the dependence of each field on the underlying characteristic measure.
1
1

1

In particular, our results imply multipoint convergence of their field
\widehat{F}_{N}
to (
SHE
) as well.
This is proved using an explicit coupling of the two fields via the Brownian web. See
[
Yu16
, Equation (1.8)]
and the subsequent discussion for a short proof, which in turn is based on a construction of
[
SSS14
, Section 3]
.

Note that for the case where the characteristic measure is a multiple of the uniform measure, we have
\mu=\nu
. Proposition 1.22 in
[
BR20
]
provides exact moment formulas for the unscaled version of
F_{N}(t,x)
in this case. Taking the scalings into account we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x)F_{N}(t,y)]

\displaystyle=\oint_{r_{1}+i\mathbb{R}}\oint_{r_{2}+i\mathbb{R}}\frac{N^{-1/2}(z_{2}-z_{1})}{N^{-1/2}(z_{2}-z_{1})-N^{-1/2}-N^{-3/4}(z_{1}+z_{2})-N^{-1}z_{1}z_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cdot e^{\frac{t}{2}(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2})+(xz_{1}+yz_{2})}\frac{N^{-1/2}}{(N^{-1/4}+N^{-1/2}z_{1})(N^{-1/4}+N^{-1/2}z_{2})}\frac{dz_{1}}{2\pi i}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i},

where
r_{1},r_{2}
are fixed numbers not depending on
t,x,y
and
N
(large enough), and such that
r_{2}>r_{1}+1.

Now assume that we have a sequence
(t^{N},x^{N},y^{N})
converging to
(t,x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Note that for
z_{1}\in r_{1}+i\mathbb{R}
and
z_{2}\in r_{2}+i\mathbb{R}
we have that
|e^{\frac{t^{N}}{2}(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2})+(x^{N}z_{1}+y^{N}z_{2})}|\leq\mathrm{C}e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2})}
for some constant
\mathrm{C}>0
which is independent of
N,z_{1},z_{2}
. Also, note that the ratios appearing in the integral may be bounded independently of
N
thanks to the fact that
z_{1}\in r_{1}+i\mathbb{R}
and
z_{2}\in r_{2}+i\mathbb{R}
with
r_{2}>r_{1}+1
. Therefore, by the dominated convergence theorem (with dominating function given by
e^{-\frac{1}{\mathrm{C}}(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2})}
) we may conclude that the integral expression for
\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t^{N},x^{N})F_{N}(t^{N},y^{N})]
converges as
N\to\infty
to

\oint_{r_{1}+i\mathbb{R}}\oint_{r_{2}+i\mathbb{R}}\frac{z_{2}-z_{1}}{z_{2}-z_{1}-1}e^{\frac{t}{2}(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2})+(xz_{1}+yz_{2})}\frac{dz_{1}}{2\pi i}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}.

This is known to agree with
\mathbf{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{t}(x)\mathcal{Z}_{t}(y)]
, see
[
BC14
, Section 6.2]
, thus proving the uniform convergence.
∎

With the above lemma in place, we now prove a stronger version of Theorem
1.7
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Suppose that the deterministic sequence of vectors
(x^{N}_{1},\ldots,x^{N}_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
converges as
N\to\infty
to
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Fix
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}>0
. Then we have the joint convergence

\big(F_{N}(t_{i},x^{N}_{i}))_{i=1}^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\big(\mathcal{Z}_{t_{i}}(x_{i})\big)_{i=1}^{m}.

Proof.

We first prove the case when
x_{i}^{N}=x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
. We give a proof for
m=1
to simplify the notation, but the generalization to larger
m
is straightforward. We will simply write
(t_{1},x_{1})
as
(t,x)
, which will be fixed throughout the proof. By Lemma
7.1
we get that
\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x)]\leq(2\pi t)^{-1/2}
which is independent of
N
, thus it follows that the
\{F_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
are tight. Consider any limit point
\mu_{0}
of the laws of
\{F_{N}(t,x)\}_{N\geq 1}
. Fix a smooth compactly supported nonnegative function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
which integrates to
1
, and define
\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)=\lambda^{-1}\phi(\lambda^{-1}(y-x)).
By Proposition
7.2
we know that for each
\lambda,x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\big\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)F_{N}(t,y)dy\big\}_{N\geq 1}
is a tight sequence. For
r\geq 1
, consider any measure
\mu_{r}
on
\mathbb{R}^{r+1}
which is a joint limit point as
N\to\infty
of

\bigg(F_{N}(t,x)\ ,\ \big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi^{2^{-k}}_{x}(y)F_{N}(t,y)dy\big)_{k=1}^{r}\bigg),

such that the first marginal of
\mu_{r}
is
\mu_{0}
. Using a diagonalization argument, these measures
\mu_{r}
may be chosen so that they form a projective family, and therefore by the Kolmogorov extension theorem, we may consider any projective limit
\mu
which will be a measure on the space of sequences
(a_{k})_{k\geq 0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the projection maps. By Proposition
7.2
, we find that for such a measure
\mu
the marginal distribution of
(a_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is simply equal to the law of
\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Z}_{t}(y)\phi^{2^{-k}}_{x}(y)dy\big)_{k\geq 1}
. We now claim that

(7.2)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\lambda\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg|F_{N}(t,x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}F_{N}(t,y)\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy\bigg|=0.

Assuming this fact, one finds that such a measure
\mu
is necessarily supported on those sequences
(a_{k})_{k\geq 0}
which satisfy
a_{0}=\lim_{k\to\infty}a_{k}
in
L^{1}(\mu)
, which means that
a_{0}
must have the law of
\mathcal{Z}_{t}(x)
under
\mu
. We are thus left to check (
7.2
).
By Lemma
7.3
, we have that

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[(F_{N}(t,x)-F_{N}(t,y))^{2}]=\mathbf{E}[(\mathcal{Z}_{t}(x)-\mathcal{Z}_{t}(y))^{2}]\leq\mathrm{C}|x-y|

uniformly over compacts sets of
(t,x,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
where the above inequality is a known estimate for (
SHE
) (see
[
Das24
, Proposition 2.4-nw]
for example). Thus, by Fatou’s lemma, we note that

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg|F_{N}(t,x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}F_{N}(t,y)\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}|F_{N}(t,x)-F_{N}(t,y)|\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}|F_{N}(t,x)-F_{N}(t,y)|\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[(F_{N}(t,x)-F_{N}(t,y))^{2}]^{1/2}\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x-y|^{1/2}\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy=\mathrm{C}\lambda^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u|^{1/2}\phi(u)du.

Taking
\lambda\to 0
leads to (
7.2
). To justify the above application of Fatou’s lemma with
\limsup
, one needs a bound on the integrand which is independent of
N
and is in
L^{1}(\phi^{\lambda}_{x}(y)dy)
, for which we may use Lemma
7.1
to get

\mathbb{E}|F_{N}(t,x)-F_{N}(t,y)|\leq\mathbb{E}[F_{N}(t,x)+F_{N}(t,y)]\leq 2(2\pi t)^{-1/2}.

Finally, the case when
x_{i}^{N}
varies with
N
follows from the fact that as
N\to\infty
we have

\mathbb{E}\big[\big(F_{N}(t_{i},x_{i}^{N})-F_{N}(t_{i},x_{i})\big)^{2}\big]\to 0

due to the uniform convergence in Lemma
7.3
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

We remark that one can allow
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}
to depend on
N
as well, and the multi-point convergence result analogous to Proposition
7.4
would still be true as long as the
t_{i}^{N}
converge to some positive real numbers
t_{i}
as
N\to\infty
. These are the precise modifications needed: first one modifies the proof of Proposition
7.2
to allow the
t_{i}
to depend on
N
. This is fairly immediate since Theorem
1.4
(b)
gives convergence in a topology that is uniform in time. Then notice that in Lemma
7.3
we have already allowed the
t_{i}
to depend on
N
. Thus we can replace
t_{i}
by
t_{i}^{N}
in Proposition
7.4
as well (the estimate using Fatou’s Lemma will still work), which is enough to establish the claim. After the claim is established, some interesting effects can be observed by setting
t^{N}_{i}:=t_{i}+\alpha N^{-1/4}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Using Definition
1.6
one sees in this case that
\alpha
gets reinterpreted as a shift of the
x
variable, but with different scaling values.

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

We continue with the same notations as in the statement of the Theorem
1.8
. Fix any
a\in\mathbb{R}
.
Set
v:=\sqrt{ct}
and
x:=d\sqrt{\frac{t}{c}}
so that
c=\frac{v^{2}}{t}
and
d=vx/t
. It suffices to show that

(7.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{P}_{SB^{(k)}_{\nu}}\bigg(\max_{1\leq i\leq k(N)}\big\{X^{i}(Nt)\big\}-vN^{3/4}-x\sqrt{N}

\displaystyle-\tfrac{tN^{1/4}}{v}\big(r_{N}-\tfrac{1}{4}\log N\big)\leq aN^{1/4}\bigg)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}(t/v[G+\log\mathcal{Z}_{v^{2}/t}(vx/t)]\leq a)},

where
\mathcal{Z}
solves (
SHE
) with
\sigma=\frac{v}{{2}t\nu([0,1])}
and
G
is an independent Gumbel random variable.

For now, fix any
k\in\mathbb{N}
which may be arbitrary. Fix a realization of the kernels
\omega=\{K_{s,t}:-\infty<s<t<\infty\}.
As before, we let
\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(k)}
be the quenched probability of
k
random motions
(X^{i}(t))_{i=1}^{k}
sampled from these kernels. By the scale invariance of SBM kernels, the random variable

\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(k)}\bigg(\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\big\{X^{i}(Nt)\big\}\leq vN^{3/4}+x\sqrt{N}+\frac{t}{v}N^{1/4}(r_{N}-\frac{1}{4}\log N)+aN^{1/4}\bigg)

has the same law for any
y>0
as the random variable

\mathbb{P}_{\omega}^{(k)}\bigg(\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\big\{X^{i}(Ny^{2}t)\big\}\leq y\big[vN^{3/4}+x\sqrt{N}+\frac{t}{v}N^{1/4}(r_{N}-\frac{1}{4}\log N)+aN^{1/4}\big]\bigg)

except that the characteristic measure in the latter expression has changed to
y\cdot\nu
. Now we make a specific choice of
y:=v/t
and we get by Definition
1.6
that it is equal (pathwise, not just in distribution) to the quantity

(7.4)

\displaystyle\bigg(1-\exp\bigg[-\frac{v^{2}}{2t}\sqrt{N}-N^{\frac{1}{4}}\frac{vx}{t}-\frac{av}{t}-r_{N}\bigg]\cdot F_{N}\big(v^{2}/t,\;\tfrac{vx}{t}+N^{-\frac{1}{4}}(\tfrac{av}{t}+r_{N}-\tfrac{1}{4}\log N)\big)\bigg)^{k}.

where it is implicit that the characteristic measure associated to
F_{N}
is
(v/t)\cdot\nu
. Then we may choose

k=k(N)=\bigg\lfloor\exp\bigg[\frac{v^{2}}{2t}\sqrt{N}+\frac{vx}{t}N^{1/4}+r_{N}\bigg]\bigg\rfloor

as stated in the theorem to obtain that (
7.4
) is a quantity of the form
(1-\frac{u(N)}{k(N)+O_{N}(1)})^{k(N)}
, where the
O_{N}(1)
term is deterministic and bounded between
0
and
1
, and
u(N)
is a sequence of random variables which by Proposition
7.4
converges in law to the strictly positive random variable
e^{-\frac{av}{t}}\mathcal{Z}_{v^{2}/t}(vx/t)
. Since the functions
u\mapsto(1-\frac{u}{k(N)+O_{N}(1)})^{k(N)}
converge uniformly to
u\mapsto e^{-u}
on compact subsets of
[0,\infty)
, we can conclude that the expression in (
7.4
), therefore converges in law to the random variable

\exp\big[-e^{-\frac{av}{t}}\mathcal{Z}_{v^{2}/t}(vx/t)\big],

where
\mathcal{Z}
solves (
SHE
) with
\sigma=\frac{v}{{2}t\nu([0,1])}
. Now this convergence in law also implies convergence of the associated annealed expectations thanks to the boundedness of all quantities involved, which implies that the limit of the left side of (
7.3
) equals

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\exp\big[-e^{-\frac{av}{t}}\mathcal{Z}_{v^{2}/t}(vx/t)\big]\bigg],

which agrees with the right-hand side of (
7.3
) by the explicit form of the Gumbel distribution.
∎

Using Remark
7.5
, one could also consider replacing time
Nt
by time
Nt
plus a correction of order
N^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha<1
, and this would change the constants appearing in the proof in an explicit manner and introduce even more terms in the recentering if
\alpha\in[1/2,1)
. For brevity, we will not explore this.

Appendix A
Technical lemmas on Brownian bridges

Definition A.1

(Brownian concatenated process)
.

Fix any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and
s\in[0,1]
. Let
B_{\operatorname{br}}
be a Brownian bridge on
[0,s]
from
0
to
x
. Let
W
be a standard Brownian motion independent of
B_{\operatorname{br}}
. We define the Brownian concatenated process
B_{s,x}
with anchor at
(s,x)
as

\displaystyle B_{s,x}(y):=\begin{cases}B_{\operatorname{br}}(y)&y\in[0,s]\\
W(y-s)+x&y\in[s,1].\end{cases}

Lemma A.2
.

Let
W=(W_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}
be a Brownian motion on
[0,1]
. Let
F=(F_{s})_{s\in[0,1]}:C[0,1]\to C[0,1]
be a Borel-measurable functional. Suppose that
\mathbf{E}[\sup_{s\leq 1}F_{s}(W)^{2}]<\infty
. Suppose further that the function
g(s,x):=\mathbf{E}\left[F_{s}(B_{s,x})\right]
is bounded on
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}
and continuous in
x
for each
s>0
. Then for all compactly supported smooth functions
\phi
we have the identity

\mathbf{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{1}F_{s}(W)\phi(W_{s})ds\bigg]=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(s,x)p_{s}(x)\phi(x)\,dx\,ds,

and moreover for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\mathbf{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{1}F_{s}(W)dL_{x}^{W}(s)\bigg]=\int_{0}^{1}g(s,x)p_{s}(x)ds,

where
dL^{W}_{x}(s)
is the Lebesgue-Stiltjes measure associated to the nondecreasing function
s\mapsto L_{x}^{W}(s).

We remark that all expectations appearing above are finite. Let
Z:=\sup_{s\leq 1}|F_{s}(W)|
. Note that
\big|\int_{0}^{1}F_{s}(W)\phi(W_{s})ds\big|\leq Z\cdot\int_{0}^{1}|\phi(W_{s})|ds
and
\big|\int_{0}^{1}F_{s}(W)dL_{x}^{W}(s)\big|\leq Z\cdot L^{W}_{x}(1).
Therefore a simple application of Cauchy-Schwarz (noting that
\mathbf{E}[Z^{2}]<\infty
by assumption) shows that all expectations above are finite.

Proof.

Note that for all
\phi
continuous and compactly supported, by the tower property of conditional expectation we have

\mathbf{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{1}F_{s}(W)\phi(W_{s})ds\bigg]=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{E}[F_{s}(W)\phi(W_{s})]ds=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{E}\big[\mathbf{E}[F_{s}(W)|W_{s}]\phi(W_{s})\big]ds.

Now the law of
W
conditioned on
W_{s}
is precisely given by the Brownian concatenated process with anchor at
(s,W_{s}).
Thus,
\mathbf{E}[F_{s}(W)|W_{s}]=g(s,W_{s})
. Therefore, the above expectation equals

\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{E}[g(s,W_{s})\phi(W_{s})]ds=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(s,x)p_{s}(x)\phi(x)dxds,

proving the first identity. To prove the second identity, we will let
\phi
converge weakly to a Dirac mass and use the continuity in
x
of
g
. Let
\phi
be smooth, nonnegative, even, supported in
[-1,1]
, and suppose that it integrates to 1. Let
\phi^{\varepsilon}(u):=\varepsilon^{-1}\phi(\varepsilon^{-1}u)
. It is standard that the random measure on
[0,1]
given by
\phi^{\varepsilon}(x-W_{s})ds
converges almost surely as
\varepsilon\to 0
to
L^{W}_{x}(ds),
with respect to the topology of weak convergence on the space of finite measures on
[0,1].
Consequently, since we assume continuity of
s\mapsto F_{s}(W)
we have the almost sure convergence

(A.1)

\int_{0}^{1}F_{s}(W)\phi^{\varepsilon}(x-W_{s})ds\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon\to 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}\int_{0}^{1}F_{s}(W)L^{W}_{x}(ds).

Now by the first identity, the expectation of the left-hand side equals

\int_{0}^{1}H_{x}^{\varepsilon}(s)ds,\ \ \ \mbox{ where }H_{x}^{\varepsilon}(s):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(s,u)p_{s}(u)\phi^{\varepsilon}(x-u)du.

It is clear from the continuity of
g
in
x
that
H_{x}^{\varepsilon}(s)\to g(s,x)p_{s}(x)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and moreover since
g
is assumed to be globally bounded and
\phi
is supported in
[-1,1]
, we have that

|H_{x}^{\varepsilon}(s)|\leq\max_{u\in[x-1,x+1]}|g(s,u)|p_{s}(u)\leq\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}s^{-1/2}.

Hence by the dominated convergence theorem we find that

\mathbf{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{1}F_{s}(W)\phi^{\varepsilon}(x-W_{s})ds\bigg]=\int_{0}^{1}H^{\varepsilon}(s)ds\to\int_{0}^{1}g(s,x)p_{s}(x)ds.

Thus it suffices to prove that (
A.1
) also holds in
L^{1}
. To prove that, we note that

\bigg|\int_{0}^{1}F_{s}(W)\phi^{\varepsilon}(x-W_{s})ds\bigg|\leq\int_{0}^{1}|F_{s}(W)|\phi^{\varepsilon}(x-W_{s})ds\leq Z\int_{0}^{1}\phi^{\varepsilon}(x-W_{s})ds,

where
Z:=\sup_{s\in[0,1]}|F_{s}(W)|
. By the occupation time formula for the local time, we have

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\int_{0}^{1}\phi^{\varepsilon}(x-W_{s})ds=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi^{\varepsilon}(x-u)L^{W}_{u}(1)du\leq\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}}L^{W}_{u}(1).

Summarizing the last two expressions and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have that

\mathbf{E}_{BM}\bigg[\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\bigg|\int_{0}^{1}F_{s}(W)\phi^{\varepsilon}(x-W_{s})ds\bigg|\bigg]\leq\mathbf{E}[Z^{2}]^{1/2}\mathbf{E}\big[\sup_{u\in\mathbb{R}}L^{W}_{u}(1)^{2}\big]^{1/2}.

The expected square of the sup over local times is finite, e.g., by
[
RY99
, Chapter XI, Theorem (2.4)]
. Therefore, we have uniform integrability and thus
L^{1}
convergence in (
A.1
) as desired.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
B^{s,x}(t)
be a Brownian bridge on
[0,s]
from
0
to
x
. Denote by
L^{B^{s,x}}_{0}(t)
the local time at 0 of
B^{s,x}
at time
t
. Then the map
(s,x)\mapsto L^{B^{s,x}}_{0}(s)
has a modification which is almost surely continuous in both variables on
(0,1]\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
G(s,x):=L^{B^{s,x}}_{0}(s)
. Note that the zero set of
B^{s,xs^{1/2}}
is just a reparametrization of that of
B^{x,1}
, in fact
B^{x,xs^{1/2}}
is just
s^{1/2}
multiplied by a reparametrization of
B^{x,1}
, and so we have that
G(s,xs^{1/2})=G(1,x)
. Thus
G(s,x)=G(1,xs^{-1/2})
and therefore it suffices to show a.s. Hölder continuity of
x\mapsto G(1,x)
.

To prove continuity of
x\mapsto G(1,x)
, let us simplify notation and henceforth write
B^{1,x}=B^{x}
. Now write
L_{0}^{B^{x}}(1)=L_{0}^{B^{x}}(1/2)+L_{0}^{W^{x}}(1/2)
, where
W^{x}(t)=B^{x}(1-t).
Note that on the time interval
[0,1/2]
,
B^{x}
is absolutely continuous with respect to a Brownian motion of drift
x
started from 0, and moreover the Radon-Nikodym derivative is
independent
of
x
. Likewise,
W^{x}
is absolutely continuous with respect to a Brownian motion of drift
-x
started from
x
, and similarly, the Radon-Nikodym derivative is
independent
of
x
. Consequently, for a standard Brownian motion
Z
if we define
Z^{a,\lambda}(t):=a+\lambda t+Z(t),
then it suffices to show that
(a,\lambda)\mapsto L_{0}^{Z^{a,\lambda}}(1/2)
is a.s. jointly Hölder continuous as this would imply the desired result for both
B^{x}
and
W^{x}
. Note that
L_{0}^{Z^{a,\lambda}}(1/2)=L_{-a}^{Z^{0,\lambda}}(1/2).
For Brownian motion, joint continuity of local times in the drift and the spatial variable is now a standard result.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Fix
a,b,c,d,x\in\mathbb{R}
with
c^{2}+d^{2}=1
. Let
B
be a Brownian bridge from
0
to
x
on
[0,1]
and let
W
be an independent standard Brownian motion. Consider the process
X:=a+cB+dW
. For all
\theta>0
, we have

(A.2)

\mathbf{E}[e^{\theta L_{0}^{X}(1)}]\leq\theta\sqrt{2\pi}e^{\theta^{2}/2}.

Remark A.5
.

Since the above bound is uniform in
a,b,c,d,x
, the same bound continues to hold when
a,b,c,d,x
are random variables (with
c^{2}+d^{2}=1
) independent of
B
and
W
.

Proof.

Note that conditioned on
W(1)
,
X
is a standard Brownian bridge from
a
to
a+cx+dW(1)
. In
[
Pit99
, (3)]
it is shown that for a standard Brownian bridge
Y
from
a
to
a+b
one has for all
v>0
that

\mathbb{P}(L_{0}^{Y}(1)>v)=e^{b^{2}/2}e^{-(|a|+|a+b|+v)^{2}/2}

and therefore if
\theta>0
then

\mathbf{E}[e^{\theta L_{0}^{Y}(1)}]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\theta e^{\theta y}\mathbb{P}(L_{0}^{Y}(1)>y)dy\leq\theta e^{b^{2}/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{\theta y}e^{-(|a|+|a+b|+y)^{2}/2}dy=\theta\sqrt{2\pi}e^{\theta^{2}/2}e^{-(|a|+|a+b|)\theta}.

Thus returning to the process
X
we simply take
b=cx+dW(1)
, and we find that

\mathbf{E}[e^{\theta L_{0}^{X}(1)}|W(1)]\leq\theta\sqrt{2\pi}e^{\theta^{2}/2}e^{-(|a|+|a+cx+dW(1)|)\theta}.

Taking the expectation again and noting that
e^{-(|a|+|a+cx+dW(1)|)\theta}\leq 1
we arrive at (
A.2
), proving the claim.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

Fix
r,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Fix
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{m})\in[0,1]^{m}
, and
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{r})\in[0,1]^{r}
. Let
\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}}=(B^{i})_{i=1}^{r}
be
r
-many independent processes with each
B^{i}
being distributed as a Brownian concatenated process with the anchor at
(s_{i},x_{i})
(recall Definition
A.1
). Let
\ell_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be linear functions for
1\leq k\leq m
. Define

f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}):=\mathbf{E}\bigg[\exp\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{m}L_{0}^{\ell_{k}(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})}(t_{k})\bigg)\bigg].

Then
f
is globally bounded and continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(0,1]^{r}\times(0,1]^{m}.

Proof.

We use a coupling argument based on Lemma
A.3
. Take
W^{1},\ldots,W^{n}
to be i.i.d. Brownian motions on
[0,1]
and then define

B^{j,s,x}(t):=W^{j}(t)-(\tfrac{t}{s}\wedge 1)(W^{j}(s)-x).

Note that for all
r
-tuples of pairs
(s_{j},x_{j})
the processes
(B^{j,s_{j},x_{j}})_{j=1}^{r}
are distributed as
(B^{i})_{i=1}^{r}.
Applying Lemma
A.3
we see that if
(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{s}_{n},\mathbf{t}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(0,1]^{r}\times(0,1]^{m},
we have

L_{0}^{\ell_{k}(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n}})}(t_{n,k})\to L_{0}^{\ell_{k}(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})}(t_{k})

for each
1\leq k\leq n
almost surely. Consequently, the exponential of the sum over
k
also converges almost surely. To show convergence of the expectations of those exponentials we need uniform integrability of the exponentials. We will prove this in a way that also gives global boundedness of the function
f.
Since local time is an increasing process we have the automatic bound
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t})\leq f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{1}).
To prove uniform integrability we will upper-bound
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{s},\mathbf{1})
in
L^{2}
independently of
s_{j},x_{j}
. First, apply Hölder’s inequality to obtain

\mathbf{E}\bigg[\exp\bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{m}L_{0}^{\ell_{k}(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})}(1)\bigg)\bigg]\leq\prod_{k=1}^{m}\mathbf{E}\bigg[\exp\bigg(mL_{0}^{\ell_{k}(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})}(1)\bigg)\bigg]^{1/m}.

Thus, it suffices to show each term in the product above is finite. Let us take any
\ell(\mathbf{v}):=a_{1}v_{1}+\cdots+a_{r}v_{r}
. Assume without loss of generality that
s_{1}\leq\cdots\leq s_{r}.
Then on each subinterval,
[s_{j},s_{j+1}]
, we have

\ell(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{j}a_{i}[W^{i}(t)-(W^{i}(s_{i})-x_{i})]+\sum_{i=j+1}^{r}a_{i}[W^{i}(t)-\tfrac{t}{s_{i}}(W^{i}(s_{i})-x_{i})].

Let us set

(A.3)

\displaystyle D_{1,j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{j}a_{i}^{2},\qquad D_{2,j}:=\sum_{i=j+1}^{r}a_{i}^{2},\qquad\gamma:=D_{2,j}\cdot\big[\sum_{i=j+1}^{r}a_{i}^{2}/s_{i}\big]^{-1}.

Note that
\ell(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})(t+s_{j})-\ell(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})(s_{j})
has mean
t\sum_{i=j+1}^{r}\frac{a_{i}x_{i}}{s_{i}}
, and for
s,t\in[0,s_{j+1}-s_{j}]
its covariance function is given by

\displaystyle C(s,t)

\displaystyle:=(s\wedge t)\cdot D_{1,j}+\sum_{i=j+1}^{r}a_{i}^{2}\big[(s\wedge t)-\tfrac{ts}{s_{i}}\big]=(s\wedge t)\cdot D_{1,j}+\big[(s\wedge t)-\tfrac{ts}{\gamma}\big]\cdot D_{2,j}.

As
s_{1}\leq\cdots\leq s_{r}
, one can check that
\gamma\geq(s_{j+1}-s_{j})
. Thus,

\ell(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})(t+s_{j})-\ell(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})(s_{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}U_{1}(t)+U_{2}(t)

as processes on
[0,s_{j+1}-s_{j}]
where
U_{1}
is a Brownian bridge with diffusion rate
D_{2,j}
from
0
to
\gamma\sum_{i=j+1}^{r}a_{i}x_{i}/s_{i}
on
[0,\gamma]
and
U_{2}
is an independent Brownian motion with diffusion rate
D_{1,j}
.

On
[0,s_{j+1}-s_{j}],
we have shown that
\ell(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})-\ell(\mathbf{B}^{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{x}})(s_{j})
is distributed as the independent sum of a Brownian bridge (not necessarily ending at zero) and a Brownian motion whose diffusivities sum to
\|\ell\|_{2}^{2}=a_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{r}^{2}.
Consequently, by breaking up the expectation on each of these subintervals (by again applying Hölder’s inequality) and using the fact that each of these subintervals has size at most 1, it suffices to bound
\mathbf{E}[e^{\theta L_{0}^{X}(1)}]
for all
\theta>0
where
X:=a+cB+dW
, where
B
is a standard Brownian bridge from
0
to
x
on
[0,1]
and
W
is an independent standard Brownian motion on
[0,1]
. More precisely, we need a bound which is uniform over all
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
c^{2}+d^{2}=1
. This is precisely done in Lemma
A.4
. This completes the proof.
∎

We remark that Definition
A.1
can be naturally extended to Brownian concatenated processes defined on
[0,T]
for any fixed
T>0
. Both Lemma
A.2
and Lemma
A.6
continue to hold under this setting.
